613. HENRY SCARLETT was again indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of January, 1 ring, value 2s.; 2 teaspoons, value 4s.; 1 shift, value 2s. 6d.; 1 sovereign, and 1 shilling, the property of Sarah Brown.
SARAH BROWN. I am a widow, and live at No. 72, in Old-street-road. The prisoner came to me as a lodger on the 27th of September; the last time I saw him was on the morning of the 18th of January - he told me he should return at night, but I did not see him again till he was at Worship-street - I had missed my property a long time before that.
WILLIAM ATTFIELD. I took the prisoner on the 22nd of January - he said he lived at No. 3, Old-street-road, but I received information that he was living with an aunt of his in Hoxton Old-town; I went there, and asked what room he lodged in - I was shown up stairs; I looked about for books, but saw none - I saw a gold ring on the mantel-shelf; I did not take it then, but went to Mrs. Brown's - I found forty-eight duplicates in his room; one was for the tea-spoon, and one for a shift - I told Mrs. Brown I had seen a ring at his aunt's, and she said,"That is a ring which I lost;" I went back - the ring was not where I had seen it before, but I found it in the cellar; Mrs. Brown had described it as having been studded with red stones, except in three places, in which she had put black ones.
WILLIAM BURT. I am a pawnbroker. I have a shift, pawned by the prisoner.
RICHARD LAW. I am a pawnbroker. I have a teaspoon, pawned by the prisoner on the 27th of November.
MRS. BROWN. These are my property - I also lost a sovereign and a shilling, on the 1st or 2nd of December, out of my glass drawer.
Prisoner's Defence. The ring I found.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Transported for Seven Years for each offence.
[trialAccount t18310217-188]
614. THOMAS PAPERA was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of January, 13 casts of models in plaster, value 2s. 6d., the goods of James Philip Papera and another, to whom he was servant.
JAMES PHILIP PAPERA. I am a modeller and figuremaker, in partnership with my mother, at No. 60, Marylebone-street, May-fair - the prisoner is my cousin; he is not a partner - he was employed as a workman; he had 36s. a week and his victuals. I lost several models and moulds too, but the stock is numerous, and I cannot tell when I missed them - I got a search-warrant, and went to the prisoner's; I knew where he lived, but I do not know the name of the place - I found these thirteen casts from models, which I knew to be our's; we did not find any models of legs and arms there.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. I believe the prisoner is a very skilful workman? A. Yes - my mother had recently before this received an order from Lord Onslow to execute some figures, to the amount of 110l.; the prisoner had left my mother before that order was executed - the order was given at my house, and in my name; his Lordship did not direct that the prisoner should execute his order - it was not to be executed till the spring; I had a quarrel with my mother some time ago - I went away, but not far off; I did not go to live with the prisoner - I swear that; I have a house of my own in Elms-lane, Bayswater, where I reside - I am not married; I went before the Magistrate when the prisoner was committed - I did not then give charge of him for stealing some legs; I found the cast of a leg in Brujiarty's window, in Russel-street, and stated that to my mother - it was not from me that he received that model; he borrowed some models of me - I did not state to the Magistrate that I was the person who took the cast from the lady's leg, nor did I deny it; the question was not put to me - I did not state to the Magistrate that I and my uncle together took that cast; I did not deliver the leg to Mr. Brujiarty, or, if I did, it was unknown to me; I was not at Madame Vestris' when the model was taken - I have been to May-fair, but not to that lady's house; I sent the prisoner and his father - I was not turned out of the room on account of my youth; the gentleman who came to our shop said, "Don't let that man come, he is too young, send two elderly men;" I do not know that we had been directed not to part with that leg - I cannot swear that I did not lend the model of it to Brujiarty; he borrowed some moulds of legs of me - if this one was lent, it was with other moulds, and I did not know it; my mother took out the warrant for some other property - she reduced the prisoner's wages on account of had behaviour; Lord Onslow gave his order to the prisoner to bring to my house - I cannot tell how often in last year I had been at the prisoner's house; I might have gone several times to fetch him to work - I saw some casts there, but I never saw any of those now in Court; these casts are all mutilated - the prisoner has three or four children, who are in the habit of coming backwards and forwards for money; it is the custom in our business to throw aside mutilated casts in the shop - these are the casts; this one, in its present state, is worth nothing, but we can put a head to it, and then it would be worth 2s. 6d. - my man's time in making it would be worth 6d.; we did not know of these at the time the prisoner got Lord Onslow's order; we have so many about that I cannot tell when I had seen these - I never saw any of these models at the prisoner's house before we took them; I never slept in his house - I had a quarrel with my mother about several affairs, but not about money.
Q. Now your mother is here, upon your oath was it not about money? A. No, I never was charged with taking any money - I charged the prisoner with stealing casts, not models.
COURT. Q. Have you not been on the prisoner's premises, and seen them there before? A. No, never, my Lord - this charge did not originate in a suspicion that he would get Lord Onslow's order.
JOHN ANDREWS. I am an officer. I went to the prisoner's, and found these casts among immense quantity of other things - they were on the mantel-piece in the cupboard, and different parts, not secreted in any manner.
Cross-examined. Q. Were they not broken? A. No, I believe I have broken this - they were whole when before the Magistrate.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18310217-189]

616. THOMAS WILSON was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of January, 28 quires of paper, value 1l. 5s., the goods of Charles Roworth and others.
CHARLES ROWORTH. I am a letter-press printer, and am in partnership with my two sons - we live near Bellyard. The prisoner is a total stranger to me. On the 20th of January I heard a noise - I came down, and found the prisoner in custody of one of our men, who had stopped him going out of our premises with some paper.
EDWARD LYON. I am in the employ of Mr. Roworth. I was returning to his premises, and met the prisoner carrying this paper; I followed him about fifty yards, and asked what he had there - he said some paper, and that he was going home; he requested me to take it, but I refused- he came very quietly back with me, and acknowledged that he had taken it from the middle of the warehouse.
MR. ROWORTH. This is our paper - the prisoner pointed out the place he took it from, and pleaded distress.
Prisoner's Defence. I was cast away, and travelled up to london for employ, but could not get any.

617. MARY WOODCOCK and MARY SMITH were indicted for stealing, on the 10th of February, 26 yards of cotton, value 1l., the goods of James Brown.
WILLIAM SHEATH. I live with Mr. James Brown, a linen-draper, of Shadwell. On the 10th of February the two prisoners came to his shop together, and looked at some printed cottons - I was standing by, and noticed that Smith held up several pieces rather higher than persons generally do, which concealed Woodcock very much; they still kept on together, and after some time I missed a piece of cotton from the counter, containing about sixteen yards - I looked for the Policeman, who came and found it up Woodcock's clothes, and another piece hanging to her tail; there was nothing found on Smith.
WILLIAM ROBERT GARDE. I am a Police-constable. I went to the shop, and took the prisoners; I found this piece of cotton between Woodcock's legs, and as she was going out of the shop I saw about a yard of this other hanging behind her - I found no money on either of them.
WILLIAM SHEATH. These are my employer's property- they had both bartered and looked at the goods; they pretended to buy a dress, and said they would leave 1s. on it.
WOODCOCK - GUILTY. Aged 16.

[trialAccount t18310217-192]
618. ROBERT PASSINGHAM and JOHN RAYNER were indicted for stealing, on the 16th of February, 3 handkerchiefs, value 15s., the goods of Daniel Gibbon.
THOMAS JOSEPH WEST. I am a Police-constable. On Wednesday evening last I was in Coventry-street, St. James', and saw the two prisoners at Mr. Gibbon's shop window - he is a hosier and glover; I saw Rayner go into the shop, while the other stood at the window - he returned with something in his hand, which he gave to Passingham, who put it under his coat on the left side; Hobbs took Rayner, and I took Passingham - I found these three handkerchiefs under his coat.
Cross-examined by MR. CARRINGTON. Q. About what time was this? A. Between seven and eight o'clock - it was dark, except from the gas-lights; there were no persons looking into the window at that time but the prisoners.
THOMAS HOBBS. I am a Police-constable. I was with West, and saw the two prisoners looking in at the window- I saw Rayner go into the shop, take hold of the handkerchiefs, and give them several tugs, till he got them down- he brought them out, and gave them to Passingham, who took them backwards.
Cross-examined by MR. BALL. Q. Are you quite certain as to the person of Passingham? A. Yes, I am; it was all done in less than five minutes - I was close to them- there was a waggon stopping close to the door, and I whipped round it and caught them.
THOMAS CAFFIN. I am a shopman to Mr. Daniel Gibbon. These three handkerchiefs are his property, and were hanging at his door - I saw them safe in the afternoon, and missed them in the evening.
Cross-examined by MR. CARRINGTON. Q. Have you any mark on them? A. No; I think we had the pieces of these handkerchiefs for three months - we sold five of them.
The prisoners received a good character.
PASSINGHAM - GUILTY. Aged 15.
RAYNER - GUILTY. Aged 13.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury. - Confined 3 Months.
[trialAccount t18310217-193]
619. WILLIAM MACK was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of January, 10 gallons of wine, value 10l., and 60 bottles, value 10s., the goods of John Whinery Spencer, his master.
JOHN WHINERY SPENCER. I live in Newton-street, Holborn. The prisoner was in my employ, as household-servant, for eighteen months; I had for a long time missed wine from my private wine-cellar, and on the 10th of January, when I went to get some brandy, I missed a quantity of that; I missed wine every time I went - I askedmy son if he had taken any; he said No - he then came down into the cellar, and we laid a plan to detect the thief, by putting this detonating snap; some of the wine was twenty-four years old, and some ten years old - when I returned to town the next morning the prisoner, who had been detected, said he had taken the wine, and drank it, broken the bottles, and thrown them with the other broken bottles; I had lost a key of the cellar for six months.
WILLIAM SPENCER. I knew of the snap having been put on the door, and about half-past eight o'clock in the evening on the 11th of January, I heard the prisoner go across the warehouse, and down to the wine-cellar - I heard the snap go off, and I went down and took him; he denied then that he had taken any wine, but the next morning he said he had taken it, and drank it - Mr. Spencer has lost not less than six dozen of wine, besides brandy.
Prisoner's Defence. His son is cohabiting with a lady in Burton-crescent, and he had been in the habit of sending wine there - I have been afraid to say any thing, because he acted as a sort of deputy master over me; they let me go home that night, and then took me the next morning - they got two men there to frighten me, and asked if I knew of any body else being guilty.
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18310217-194]
620. JOHN MILAN was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of December, 1 pair of shoes, value 3s., the goods of Patrick Stack.
PATRICK STACK. I lodged in Bainbridge-street, St. Giles', in the same room with the prisoner. I have known him since May last - he wanted to buy a pair of shoes of me, but I said I wanted them myself; I went out about two o'clock, on the 29th of December, and when I returned, he had gone out with my shoes - I met him on the the 25th of January, with them on his feet, and gave him in charge; I had never lent them to him - he works for the parish, at watering the streets in summer time, and at other times he sings ballads.
Prisoner's Defence. I asked him to let me have them, and he refused - I was much in want of them, and when he was going out, I asked him to lend them to me; I thought he said Yes - there were five or six persons in the room when I put them on, and I left my own under the table.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18310217-195]
621. FRANCES KEY was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of January. 1 counterpane, value 2s. 6d., and 1 bed-tick, value 6s., the goods of Thomas Parker.
THOMAS PARKER. I live in George-street, St. Giles'. The prisoner lived in a furnished room, in a house of mine in the next street - she had been there about three months. On the 31st of January I went into her room, because she owed me some rent - I went home, and told my wife to go and look over the things, which she did, and discovered that the articles stated were missing; the prisoner had taken the flock out of the tick, and news it up in a sheet - she afterwards gave me these duplicates.
GEORGE MACKEY. I live with Mr. Wells, a pawnbroker. I have a counterpane, for which I gave this duplicate.
THOMAS JEROME. I am a pawnbroker. I have a bedtick, which this is the duplicate of.(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoner put in a written Defence, stating that she had pawned the goods to pay her rent with, and intended to redeem them if she had time, and that the prosecutor kept a house of ill fame, known by the name of "Mothers Common's."
THOMAS PARKER. I keep a lodging-house, but I keep it honestly - I found 300l. in it once, and I advertized it.
Q. Do you keep such a house? A. I do, but that is no reason why I should be robbed.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18310217-196]
622. MARY BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of February, 1 cup, value 2s. 6d., and 1 saucer, value 2s. 6d., the goods of George Evans.
SARAH MOULDER. I live with Mr. George Evans, at Ealing. On the 13th of February the prisoner came begging to his gate, and went into the parlour - the house stands back, and has a garden before it; when I saw her she was at the parlour door, and I know she had been in the parlour, as the door had been shut, and it was then open - I went and took her by the gate; she had this cup and saucer, which had been taken from the parlour, one on one side, and the other on the other, in the baby's mantle.
JOHN FINCHER. I stopped the prisoner at the gate, with this cup and saucer.
Prisoner's Defence. It was distress drove me to it - it was only the day before that, I took my shift off, and sold it for 8d.; my parish is Portsmouth.
SARAH MOULDER. She had asked charity of me about a week before, but she did not on that day - I believe she lodged at Brentford.
GUILTY. Aged 43. - Confined Six Months.

623. LEONARD GIBSON was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of February, 7 lbs. of beef, value 3s., the goods of John Owen.
JOHN OWEN. I have a butcher's-shop in Fitzroy-market. About half-past ten o'clock on Saturday night, the 12th of February, I was returning from my other shop, and met the prisoner and another boy - they came in front of my shop, where my wife was weighing some mutton; my servant had just gone away - they both took hold of the piece of beef; they came right into my arms with it -I caught hold of the prisoner, and the beef; the other got away - I had seen them take the beef off my board; it weighed 7 1/2 lbs.
Prisoner. This is the first crime I ever committed - I was returning home last Christmas, and saw four men round the prosecutor's shop; I went and told him of it.
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Transported for Seven Years.

624. ALICE COOPER was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of January, 1 pair of stockings, value 5s.; 1 cap, value 3s.; 1 bracelet, value 2s.; 1 necklace, value 5s.; 2 handkerchiefs, value 3s., and 2 cups. of tea, value 6d., the goods of Ellen Shaw.
ELLEN SHAW. I live in Union-street, Middlesex-hospital. I had worked at a place at which the prisoner lived servant, and when she was out of place she offered to cometo assist me in washing - this was on the Wednesday; when she was gone I missed the articles stated in the indictment - they have all been found at her lodging, except she stockings, which she had on - she came to me again on the Saturday afterwards - I sent for an officer; she was charged with the robbery, and dented it.
WILLIAM ESKETT. I am an officer. I was sent for to the prosccutrix's room - I took the prisoner to the watch-house; she at first said she lived at 1slington, but afterwards she said it was of no use telling an untruth - she then gave me her address in Charlton-street, Marylebone; I found these things there; the stockings she had on.

628. JOHN COURT was indicted for stealing. on the 28th of December, 1 coat, value 5s., the goods of Samuel Challis; 1 coat, value 2s., and 1 shirt, value 1s., the goods of Thomas Kitchener .
SAMUEL CHALLIS. I lodged in Bainbridge-street, St. Giles, for about three months - the prisoner lodged there. On the 27th of December I had a coat given me; 1 hung it on a line - on getting up the next morning I missed it, about the dawn of that day - the prisoner had gone out at five o'clock that morning; he had lodged in the same room - I did not see him again till the 6th of January, when I met him in Buckeridge-street; I seized him - he said he was very sorry for what had occurred, and he would make it up by giving me something to eat; he was taken to the watch-house, and said he had borrowed the coat of Kitchener, who lodged in the house.
THOMAS KITCHENER. I lodge in the same room with the prosecutor. I missed a coat and shirt on the 28th of December - I have seen them in the officer's possession; the shirt was by the fire, and the prisoner asked me to lend him the coat to throw over his bed.
Prisoner. The coat and shirt were lent to me. Witness. I only lent him the coat to throw over his bed - I did not lend the shirt at all.
JOHN REEVE. I am a Police-constable. The prisoner was given to my charge - I took a coat and a shirt off his person.
Prisoner's Defence. I know nothing of Challis' coat- this coat and shirt Kitchener lent me.
GUILTY. Aged 26. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18310217-203]
629. CHARLES JARVIS was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of February, 1 picture, value 10s., and 4 picture-frames, value 2l., the goods of Josiah Morgan.
JOSEPH CLEMENTS. I am a Police-constable. On Wednesday, the 2nd of February, I was in Tottenham-place, about ten minutes past eleven o'clock at night; I saw a coach coming to the end of Tottenham-place, and saw the prisoner coming out of Southampton-court, and another man about ten yards in front of him - they were going towards the coach; the prisoner had the articles here produced - I asked what he had there; he made me no answer - I caught him, and pulled him round - the other man, and the coach then disappeared; I again asked the prisoner what he had there - he said pictures, and that the man in front of him had given him 6d. and a glass of gin to carry them to the coach; I took him to the station-house.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was the other man nearer to the coach? A. He was about ten yards in front of the prisoner; neither of them were within sixty yards of the coach - the prisoner was going towards the coach; here is one picture of His Majesty, the rest are frames and this crown and cushion - I found nothing on him suspicious.
JOSIAH MORGAN. I live at No. 51, London-street, Fitzroy-square, and am a picture-frame maker. These frames, crown and cushion, are mine, and this portrait - I missed them on Wednesday evening, the 2nd of February, between six and eight o'clock - I found the door of my shop open at eight, and the glass-case door open; I had seen them safe at dusk, about six o'clock.
Cross-examined. Q. Then these things must have been gone before eight o'clock? A. Yes.
Prisoner's Defence. I told him to catch hold of the man before me, as he had given me the goods to carry.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18310217-204]
630. WILLIAM CARTER was indicted for stealing. on the 27th of January, 1 bevil, value 1s. 6d., the goods of John Brickell.

On the 27th of January I looked through my window, saw the prisoner take the bevil, and put it into his fob - I let him go two doors, then went, and took him back; he denied having it, and at the same time it dropped from his trousers.
SAMPSON CHAMPION. I am a Police-constable. Theprisoner was given into my custody - I found nothing on him, but a brass cock.(Property produced and sworn to.)

[trialAccount t18310217-205]
631. WILLIAM DOBSON was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of February, I handkerchief, value 3s., the goods of John Walter Walker.
JOHN WALTER WALKER. I live at Park-house, Turnham-green, Middlesex. On the evening of the 5th of February I was walking with my wife, in Drury-lane; a constable gave me information - I put my hand to my pocket, and missed my handkerchief - I have since seen it produced by Dowding, and to the best of my belief it was mine, but I cannot swear to it, because it was lent me by a friend; I had one in my pocket, and when the officer called my attention to it it was gone.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I believe you had considerable difficulty in saying that it was the one you had lost? A. Yes, as it had been so short a time in my possession, I could not conscientiously swear to it; I stated so to the Magistrate in the first place - the officer had been examined then; he then added that he saw the man take it from my pocket, having before said he found it - I said I could not swear to it, and the Magistrate was about to discharge the prisoner; the officer then said there was no occasion to discharge him, for he saw him take it from my pocket - the officer had not been examined first; I know Dowding was sworn - I do not know whether Groom was.
COURT. Q. You were not aware your handkerchief was lost till your attention was called to it; you then missed it, and before the Magistrate you expressed considerable doubt about it? A. No; I said I could tell it by a stain which I recognized, and I pointed out that stain, but I said I would not swear to it.
GEORGE DOWDING. I live at No. 15, Rose-street, Long-acre, and am a constable. A little after seven o'clock in the evening, on the 5th of February, I was in Drury-lane, near Long-acre; I saw the prosecutor walking with a lady, and I saw the prisoner, whom I had not known before, in company with another person - i saw the prisoner touch the gentleman's pocket several times, and at last he put his hand into his right-hand coat pocket, and took out a silk hankderchief; he ran across towards Groom, and I called to him to stop him - the prisoner threw the handkerchief into the road; I took it up, and told the prosecutor he had lost his handkerchief - he identified it as his property.
Cross-examined. Q. He knew it at once? A. Yes, he had no doubt of its being his property; he identified it both to me, and at the office - he pointed out a stain, and swore he had no doubt of it, but the fact is, the gentleman did not wish to prosecute, as there was so much trouble attending it.
Q. Now, which is the truth - did he swear to it or did he not? A. He did swear to it to the best of my knowledge, and I believe that was taken down; I think I was examined after the prosecutor - I saw the prisoner make two or three attempts at the prosecutor's pocket; I was across the road - I saw him for five minutes; I am an officer - I am serving in my own right this year; I served for five years as a substitute - I am a tailor; it all helps to bring in a crust to support my family - I am both a master and a journeyman tailor; I work as journeyman for Mr. Stringer, in Long-acre; I generally get about 10l. for acting as a subsitute - I had a good many cases here last year, but I only had my expences in one case at one Session.
JOHN GROOM. I am a constable. On the evening of the 5th of February I was in Drury-lane; I saw the prisoner, and another person with him, following a lady and this gentleman - Dowding called out "Groom;" and I saw the prisoner running from the lady and gentleman with a handkerchief in his hand - he dropped the handkerchief, and I told Dowding to take it up, and to run after the lady and gentleman; I took the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you show the gentleman the handkerchief, and did he say it was his? A. Yes, he had no doubt of it; and at the office he said it was one he had borrowed of a friend - I am in no business; I did not see any more persons about the place but the prisoner and his companion; I had known the prisoner before - his clothes had no pockets - I am sure the prosecutor said he had no doubt it wasthe same handkerchief.
Prisoner. I have the same clothes on now; I have pockets in them.
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18310217-206]
632. THOMAS FRANCIS was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of January, I handkerchief, value 6d., and 1 half-sovereign, the property of David Walker.
SARAH WALKER. I am the wife of David Walker; we live in Collingwood-street, Ratcliff; the prisoner lodged there for seven months. On the 17th of January I sent him with a newspaper to a neighbouring public-house, and he did not return; soon after he was gone I missed a half-sovereign from a cupboard in a room, which I had seen safe half an hour or twenty minutes before -I in general keep the room locked, but having no one in the house but the prisoner I had not locked it that day -I do not recollect that he had been in that room; I had not sent him there - I never saw him again till the officer brought him, which was more than a week afterwards; I asked how he could have a heart to rob a woman who had been so kind to him; he said he put the half-sovereign into his pocket and lost it, and he had been to Gravesend to try to get a ship, but could not - I missed a handkerchief, and found a duplicate of it.
GEORGE BLAKE. I am a Police-constable. On the 25th of January, in consequence of information, I took the prisoner at Cock-hill; I asked if he knew any thing of Mrs. Walker - he said Yes; I asked if he knew any thing of the half-sovereign - he said Yes, that he had not changed lit but lost it, and that he had just returned from Gravesend.
WILLIAM LANCE. I am a pawnbroker. I have a cotton handkerchief pawned by the prisoner, on the 17th of January, for 3d.; it is worth about 6d.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I took the half-sovereign, put it into my pocket and lost it; I went to Gravesend - I had only 1½d. on the handkerchief.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutrix and Jury.
Judgment Respited .
[trialAccount t18310217-207]
633. CHARLES HAYNES was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 16th of January, 2 horses, price 20l., and 1 mare, price 10l., the property of William Elson, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, & c.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM ELSON. I live at Bramshot, near Liphook, in Hampshire. On the 14th of January I had three pony horses and a mare - one of the horses was brown, another a dark brown, and the third a black stallion; the mare was brown - on that day they had been employed in carrying stones on the road, and I put them up about four o'clock at night; I saw them safe in the stable between seven and eight - I got up in the morning between five and six, found the stable door broken, thrown open, and the mare, the black horse, and one of the brown ones were gone; as soon as it got day-light I looked about, and tracked two men on a dung-hill, and off again; I also tracked the feet of three borses behind a cart, up the hill, where they had turned off towards the common, and a cart appeared to have been tied to a hedge - the horses appeared to have been tied behind the cart, and to have hung back when they first started - I followed the track on for ten miles in the London road, towards Godalming; I afterwards came to London, where I saw Pople and Curtis - I missed the horses on a Saturday morning, and came to London on the Thursday following; I went to Union-ball, and saw the officers - they produced the two horses and the mare which I had lost, all three together; I am quite sure they are my horses, and were what I had seen safe in my stable.
SARAH KITCHING. I am the wife of Thomas Kitching - he is a publican, and keeps the Bee Hive public-house, at Hayes, or Yeading - I know the prisoner; he had slept in our house. On the morning of Saturday, the 15th of January, I went to the door about eleven, or between eleven and twelve o'clock, and saw the prisoner standing at our stable door - he had a black pony, with a long tail, with him; at the same time I saw a person go by in a cart - it was in consequence of what I saw that person do that I looked to the stable door, and saw the prisoner - when the prisoner had put the pony into the stable, he came into my house, with the saddle on his arm - he had something to drink; I believe a small glass of brandy; he then went away, but came again in the course of the day, slept there that night, and came down stairs the next morning while we were at breakfast; I had noticed the man with the cart had another horse tied at the side - my son came home late on that Saturday night; I saw the same pony which I had seen at the stable door, in the possession of the officer in London - I have not the least doubt of its being the same; I know a cottage near the Bee Hive, in which Moffatt, who I had seen in the cart, and a man named Holding, live - they were acquainted with the prisoner; I had seen them altogether often - the prisoner, Moffat, Holding, and two other friends had supped together at our house on that Saturday night.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you say at the last trial that you had seen these men often together? A. I will not swear that I did - it was in the middle of the day that I saw him with the pony by our stable door; our house stands a little way back from the public road, but it joins it - he could not have got to the stable without going along the public road; I think I had known him three months before: I do not know whether he is in the habit of buying and selling horses - I think I had seen him with a horse once before, but he generally came to Moffat's, and walked from there to our house - we do not keep an ostler; the prisoner gave me no charge to keep it secret - the pony was put into the stable, to which travellers or strangers might have access.
THOMAS KITCHING. JUN. I am the son of Mrs. Kitching. I came home on Saturday night, the 15th of January, between six and seven o'clock - I remember seeing the prisoner next morning up against our door; he had a black pony, with a long tail; he called out to me, "Who is the boy for a ride;" I said, "Yes, if you like" - he helped me on the pony, and told me to take him down to my father's orchard, which I did, and the prisoner followed me; I took the halter off the pony, turned him loose, and the prisoner told me to drive him up amongst my father's horses, which I did - I saw that pony afterwards in the stable by Union-hall; I am sure it was the same pony - I think it was the week afterwards.
Cross-examined. Q. Were there any white spots about this black pony? A. I did not take notice of that in particular - I did not observe whether there was a large white spot on one of his sides, nor whether the left ear was cropped, or whether he had two white fore-legs; but I know the horse I saw afterwards was the one I rode I did not notice whether he had a white spot on his forehead, but I am sure the horse I saw was the same I rode- it was about fifteen miles from our house to where I saw him again in town; I should have known him any where, but not by any particular mark; he was not a very remarkable horse - I did not say at Union-hall I would not swear to the horse, not to my recollection; I cannot swear I did not - I had seen the prisoner about twice before that day; I knew his name was Charles; he had been at my father's on the Saturday night and the Sunday night.
THOMAS KITCHING, SEN. I keep the Bee Hive, at Hayes. On Saturday morning, the 15th of January, I went to my stable, between seven and eight o'clock; there were no horses in the stable then - they were all at work; I went out on a journey, and returned about four in the afternoon - I went to my stable, and saw a black stallion pony, with a long tail; I afterwards saw the same pony in possession of Pople and Curtis, the officers - I know it was the same.
Cross-examined. Q. You took notice of it? A. Yes, I had seen it in my journey - I met the prisoner and Moffatt with a cart; one horse was in the shafts of the cart, one tied to the shafts, and this black pony following behind, either tied or very close to the cart - there was no man in the cart; Moffatt was behind the cart, and the prisoner about twenty yards behind him - I do not know where Liphook is, but I met them on the road between the Uxbridge and the Bath roads, about ten o'clock on the Saturday morning; I did not see them talk together, but they were going in a direction towards Moffatt's house - I am sure there were only two men; I had known the prisoner about two months - he had slept at my house abouta week before; I had never seen him buy or sell horses - he had put one up; I did not take particular notice of the left ear of the pony - the whole ear was not off, there might be a bit of it off; it was a white-faced pony - the greater part of his face was white; I did not see that he had white legs - I did not notice the two fore-legs, but it was a stallion - it had a long tail, a white face, and the pony I saw at Union-hall was the same pony; the legs might have been all white for what I know - the tall was not white; the prisoner slept at my house on the Saturday night, and had a cup of tea there on Sunday morning - I saw no secrecy about him.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. This was about ten o'clock? A. Yes - I saw the pony in possession of the officer the next day, and knew it to be the same.
RICHARD POPLE. I am an officer of Union-hall. I heard of the loss of Elson's horses, and in consequence of information, I went down to Hayes, with Curtis, on the 16th of January - we went to a cottage, about one hundred yards from the Bee Hive; I understand that is about forty miles from Lipbook, but I do not know it - when we got to the cottage, Curtis went in first; the door opens into the kitchen, and I saw a bay mare standing before the fireplace - I heard a noise backwards, and ran to the back door; I saw a woman holding her hand out - I saw the prisoner running across the orchard, and Curtis after him; Curtis had a pair of pistols. and I heard him threaten to shoot the prisoner if he did not stop - the prisoner had got over a gate and a ditch into the road; I returned to the kitchen - the mare was still there; I looked and saw some hair about, and I saw the mare had been cropped in the fetlocks - I then went to the stable belonging to the house, and found the two stallions; the prisoner, the two stallions, and the mare, were all brought to London - they all had long tails; Mr. Elson saw them, and claimed them.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see nobody else at the cottage, but the woman and the prisoner? A. I saw no other person in the cottage - I saw a man outside the door; I did not speak to him - I will swear I did not say to him."Did not I see you somewhere before?" I do not recollect that Curtis spoke to him - he might, and I not hear it; I was not asked on the last trial any thing about seeing the mare outside - he was about twenty yards from the road; he was perhaps ten or twenty yards from the cottage - I think he was coming from it; I did not see him come out of it - I really do not recollect seeing Curtis at the moment I saw that man; he might be engaged in the stable - he did not speak to the man in my presence; I saw a chaise on the premises - I did not ask that man whose it was, but I think I asked the prisoner.
ROBERT CURTIS. I am an officer of Union-hall. I went with Pople to Hayes on the 16th of January - we went to a cottage in the neighbourhood of the Bee Hive; I went in first - I saw a mare in the room; I saw a woman in a passage leading to another room, wave her hand, as if to some person in the back part of the house - I ran to the back door, and saw the prisoner crossing an orchard; he went through a hedge, and then over a gate, into the road- I called to him. presented a pistol, and said I would shoot him if he did not stop; I took him back to the kitchen, and saw a trifle of hair had been clipped off from the fetlock joint of the mare - we then went to the stable, where I saw two ponies; one was brown, and the other black, with a streak of white down the face - we took them and the mare to London, showed them to Elson on the Thursday following, and he identified them as his property; Mrs. Kitching saw the black pony, and said she believed it was the one she had seen at the stable. and Mr. Kitching said it was the same; when we got to the Adam and Eve, about a quarter of a mile from the Bee Hive, where my horse and chaise was, the prisoner said he did not live at the cottage; I asked where he did live - he said he lodged at the Bee Hive; I asked him when - he said,"The whole of last week"
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see any man at the cottage, or near the cottage, where you saw the woman? A. yes, there were persons in the road - I saw a man, and spoke to him, but did not ask him any question; I saw a chaise in the yard belonging to the stable.
Q. Did you ever say you believed this man stole the pony? A. No, I believe he was in league with others -I never did say I believed he stole the pony individually.
Q. Did you ever swear it? now be careful, for your own sake, A. No, I never was asked the question; I never said that at the time the pony was stolen he was passing through the turnpike-gate, in a direction from the place where it was stolen from; I said three men were seen, I did not say he was one of them - it was before the prisoner was tried for stealing them, that I said I could prove three men passed through the turnpike-gate.
Q. Did you ever say this, or any thing like it, that you could bring evidence to prove that the prisoner was seen coming from the road leading from Liphook towards the Bee Hive? A. I have said that witnesses could prove that three men were seen - I do not know the prisoner was one; I never said I could have witnesses to prove that the prisoner was seen on the road - I said three men were seen at a village below.
Q. Was it not upon your swearing at Union-ball that you believed the prisoner stole the horses, that he was remanded? A. No, he was remanded on the charge of having committed divers felonies and horse-stealing, in the County of Hants.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you afterwards show the prosecutor the horses? A. Yes, and he identified them.
COURT to THOMAS KITCHING, SEN. Q. You have stated that on the morning of the 15th of January, you met Moffatt with a cart, a horse tied to the shaft, and another tied behind? A. I will not say he was tied - he was very close to the cart; that was between two and three miles from my house, and it was about ten o'clock as near as I can guess.
COURT to ROBERT CURTIS. Q. Did you at any time state that you believed this man stole the horse? A. I believed that till I was convinced, by what passed in the other Court, that it was not so.
COURT to MRS. KITCHING. Q. Could not the prisoner as easily have taken the pony to the house where the mare was found, as he could have brought him to your house? A. Yes, it is about the same little distance from the road.
Prisoner's Defence. My Lord and Gentlemen of the Jury - I leave it to them to answer whether I should have taken the pony to the Bee Hive, if I had known it was stolen - I am quite innocent.
GUILTY. Aged 24. - Transported for Fourteen Years.

634. JOHN MASTERS was charged, on the Coroner's inquisition only, for killing and slaying Julia Masters .
MR. GEORGE DUPLEX, a surgeon, and MR. DALEY, his assistant, deposed, that in their opinion the deceased had died from"pure apoplexy," and that there were no markwhatever of recent external violence, the witnesses were, therefore, not examined.

[trialAccount t18310217-209]
Before Mr. Baron Bailey,
635. JOHN BARRETT was indicted for feloniously assaulting John Shannon, and with a certain sharp instrument, unlawfully, wilfully, and unaliciously cutting him, with intent to kill and murder him.
SECOND COUNT, stating his intent to be to do him some grievous bodily harm.
JOHN SHANNON. I came from Limerick, in lreland, and work for the Corporation of the Trinity-house. - I have known the prisoner almost ever since he was born. Last Saturday night three weeks I went into the Rose and Crown, at Wapping, between six and seven o'clock; the prisoner was there before I went in, and left before me - I had been in no quarrel there; I went home between nine and ten; I sent my wife out for something for supper - the window happened to be open, and within two doors of my house I heard my child, who is about ten years old, make an alarm that his mother was killed outside; I live on the first floor - I ran down into the street; the child had made a report that there were three people, but I saw only the prisoner and my wife - I said to the prisoner, "Is that you, you villain, Jack Barrett, that is making a row, and using my wife so?" he made no answer; he stooped down, as I thought, for a stone, and cut me either with a knife or razor between the calf of my leg and my ancle, at the back of my leg; I might have touched him, but never struck him, nor did I go towards him in a menacing manner, nor had I touched him roughly - I had not touched him before I was cut; we never had any words since we were born - I know of no ill-will on his part towards me; when I found I was cut I went home, and was carried from there to the doctor's - I was afterwards carried to the London-hospital, and have been brought from there this morning; I am still lame - I cannot stand on my leg.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you mean to say you went home immediately you were cut? A. I do - I never struck the prisoner at all; he did not call Murder! I saw nobody come to his assistance - I saw him laying down on the ground, about ten minutes after he struck me; I had come out, and gone along the wall to give him in charge - he might have called for assistance before I came up; I was not on the top of him - I was tried at Hick's-hall about nine years ago for a common assault; I saw Maloney in the street - he and Flannigan took me to the doctor's; they did not rescue the prisoner from me - I was not on him, nor beating him; Flannigan was three or four perches off when this occurred.
CATHERINE SHANNON. I am the prosecutor's wife. He came home on this Saturday night - he was not in liquor; he sent me out for some supper, and when I got down I and a young Irish woman, named Mrs. Angledon, an acquaintance of the prisoner's, had some words within three or four doors of my house; she made an offer to hit me, and when she made that offer she fell - I did not strike at her; the prisoner then came up to me, hit me in the breast, and while I was down that woman's husband came and gave me a kick - I hallooed out Murder! I had not seen that woman for some weeks before; when they heat me and I fell, my cloak went over my face, and I did not see what happened to my husband, nor hear him say any thing - when I got up I went up stairs to my children, who were crying; whether my husband was hurt then I cannot tell - I heard no noise in the street; my boy came up in about five minutes, and said his father had been hurt - when I came down stairs the second time my husband was taken to Dr. Benson's; I went there, and he was taken to the London-hospital the same night by Maloney and John Flannigan, in a truck.
Cross-examined. Q. How long were you up stairs before you came down? A. About ten minutes - I did not see the prisoner then, I saw my husband; I had had part of two pints of beer between me, my husband, and another man and his wife, at the Dolphin, about nine o'clock - I was not at the Rose and Crown; my children were in bed, except the infant which I had in my arms - my husband had no row that night, to my knowledge; he came straight from the pay-table - I swear I heard of no row between him and a man named Russel, outside the Rose and Crown; I went to the Dolphin with him about eight o'clock - we staid there an hour or an hour and a half; no spirits were drank - I never struck Mrs. Angledon, nor touched her; I never saw my husband outside the Rose and Crown that night - I know he had been there, for he is paid there.
JOHN FLANNIGAN. I live at Wapping. I saw Shannon last Saturday three weeks, about ten o'clock, close to his own house; he was not wrangling nor struggling with any body that I saw - I saw him wounded by Barrett; I had been with him three or four minutes - the prisoner was not there then; he came up three or four minutes after I first met Shannon - he was not there when I first saw him; as soon as he came up Shannon made towards him - I cannot tell whether it was to lay hold of him and give him in charge, or to strike him; it appeared to me to be for one of those purposes - I think he laid his hands somewhere or other on him; he appeared to lay his hands pretty roughly on him, and said, "Is this you you villain, Jack Barrett, that has struck my wife?" and with that the prisoner stooped down and cut Shannon Shannon had been in the street three or four minutes before the prisoner came up; I did not see Mrs. Shannon - Shannon was only going on home; we were walking down the street - we had walked four or five perches; he had told me if he met the prisoner he would give him in charge - I was examined at the office; what I said was read over to me before I signed it.
Q. Did you not say there "I found the prisoner and a man named Shannon struggling together, and I went to Shannon's assistance?" A. Yes, they were struggling; when they met he went and took him, and they had a struggle - I had been walking with Shannon for three orfour minutes before; they did not meet together for two or three minutes; I saw a clasp-knife in the prisoner's hands when he came up - it was open; he was coming up against Shannon - whether they had been together before I cannot tell.
Cross-examined. Q. Was Shannon struggling with any body when you first saw him? A. Not when I met him, but when Barrett met him they were struggling together for about a minute, holding one another; Shannon had hold of the prisoner somewhere about his breast, and the prisoner held him somewhere about his waistband, but where I cannot say - Shannon thought to lay hold of him to keep him for the Police - the prisoner struggled away and cut him; they struggled about a minute before he cut him - I took the prosecutor home; Maloney took him to the doctor's, and I went back to give charge of Barrett - he ran away after cutting the prosecutor; I ran after him, tried to take him, and he cut me - I upset him; I told the Magistrate Maloney helped to take him home and to the doctor's; I do not know whether that was taken down - I had had no words with the prisoner; I had not been to his father's that night to challenge him to fight a boy named Russell - I know Russell; he had not quarrelled with the prisoner at the Rose and Crown that night in my presence; I had been at his father's that night, but did not say the prisoner should fight Russell for a sovereign, and if Russell did not beat him I would beat him well - I went there with the Police to give him in charge; I did not go before - I never said any thing of the kind; I had been drinking at the Rose and Crown that night - it is our house of call; I was there three of four hours - Barrett was there part of the time; I do not know whether Russell was there, but it is our house of call - he left about half-past nine; I cannot tell whether Shannon went with me - I was sober; he was not in my company, but might be in the room - we work in a different craft; he might leave sooner or later - I was not there when the Police were called in; I never heard of it; I was not at the Dolphin.
COURT. Q. Do you mean to say you saw the knife in his hand when first you saw him coming towards Shannon? A. Yes, and it was open; he had a small bit of bread in his hand, as I understand.
Q. Was not what you said to the Magistrate "I wanted to part them both asunder, Shannon called out that his leg was cut, I turned over to the prisoner to lay hold of him, and with that I saw a knife in his hand?" A. Yes; directly he came up I saw it in his hand - I only saw one knife in his hand that night; he came back with the very knife I saw in his hand.
Q. Did you not say with another knife? A. Yes, the Police have got that; I saw but one knife - I know where the prisoner's father lives, but do not know the name of the court; he was coming from his own home when he met Shannon; I did not see Mrs. Shannon in the street - it was about half-past ten o'clock, as far as I understand.
SARAH LOW. I live in Green Arbour-alley, nearly opposite the Police-station. I believe I saw the prisoner on Sunday morning - it was between twelve and one o'clock, nearer one; I heard the clock strike one soon after I shut my window down.
CATHERINE SHANNON. It was nearly twelve o'clock when my husband was cut - I think it was before twelve, because the shops were open; he had been up stairs for above an hour or more before this happened - the shops are shut at near twelve o'clock; my husband went home a little after ten - he sent me out in about an hour and a half.
JOHN SHANNON. This happened between eleven and twelve o'clock, as near as I can say, but I cannot say exactly, for we have no clock - I cannot say whether it was before twelve.
JOHN FLANNIGAN. The prisoner was apprehended about twelve or one o'clock - I went to his father's house with the Police; it was about an hour after this happened, as near as I can tell.
SARAH LOW re-examined. I saw the prisoner sharpen a knife between twelve and one o'clock - I was three quarters of an hour at my window, and it struck one soon after I shut it down.
Q. Your deposition states that soon after you opened your window the clock struck one? A. That is a mistake - I gave my evidence that it was between twelve and one o'clock; I saw him sharpen a knife on the step of a door; my deposition was not read over to me - I said that between twelve and one o'clock I heard a disturbance, and opened my window; I cannot tell who made the disturbance - the prisoner was one of them; I saw him with his father and mother - two Policemen were trying to take him home; he went home with them to his father's, and came out again five minutes after the Policemen left - he came up the alley, and I saw him sharpen the knife on a step three doors off; it was a moon-light - I was up two pairs of stairs, and cannot say whether he was in liquor - he went up the alley, on the green bank, and I heard a cry of Murder! three times; I believe the green bank is where Shannon was struck.
Cross-examined. Q. What office did you give your deposition at? A. The Thames Police; the Magistrate was present, but I signed it in a little room - the clerk was there; he did not read it over to me - I was sworn.
COURT. Q. Had you heard the clock strike twelve? A. Yes - I was then folding up some work, and was about five minutes before I went to my bed-room, and in ten or fifteen minutes after I went up, I heard the noise, and opened the window; I was making my bed.
JOHN EASTERBROOK. I am a Police-serjeant. Our watch-house is on the green bank - Shannon was brought there between twelve and one o'clock, (nearer one,) by Flannigan, his wife, and two or three more; he was wounded then - I had seen the prisoner about twelve, and endeavoured with his father and mother to prevail on him to go home; Robson, another Policeman, was with me - I took him part of the way down the court, and sent the other Policeman home with him; whether he went out afterwards I do not know - he was not drunk, but rather the worse for liquor; Robson had taken a clasp-knife from him; when Shannon was brought in he was cut just below the calf of the left leg; Shannon's house looks on to the green bank - I went and took the prisoner in charge about one o'clock, or near one, and took a table-knife out of his hand - I did not perceive any marks of blood on it.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you take him? A. On the first floor of his father's house - he had some breadin one hand, and the knife in the other; the knife might be in his hand for the purpose of eating his supper.
JOHN ROBSON. I am a Policeman. About a quarter-past twelve o'clock in the morning of the 30th of January, I saw the prisoner, and persuaded him to go home - his mother was there, and his father might be there, but I did not see him; he had no knife in his hand then - he was a little in liquor, but not drunk; I saw him home, and about a quarter of an hour afterwards I saw him going along about half double, into a green-grocer's shop; I could not conceive what made him go in that way - I had no reason to suppose any mischief had been done to Shannon before I saw him home the first time; when I saw him going into the green-grocer's, the landlord shoved him out of the shop - as he came up he held the knife up to me, and said, "This is the knife," holding it out to me - it was a pocket-knife; I took it out of his hand - I did not see any blood on it; I put it into my pocket, and said, "Come away home;" he used a vulgar expression, and said, "The b - r was going to murder me, but I have murdered him;" I took him about half-way home again, and in about ten minutes three of my brother officers came up with Flannigan- I took them to the prisoner's house, and took him; I cannot tell at what time Shannon received his injury - the prisoner was apprehended about five minutes past one o'clock.
JOHN FLANNIGAN. I went to apprehend the prisoner about an hour after the transaction, to the best of my knowledge.
GEORGE HAYWARD. I am a student at the London-hospital. I saw Shannon on the Sunday, and found a wound on the back part of his leg; there is every reason to expect he will have the use of his leg again, and that he will not be disabled, except by lameness, for a short time; it is not a serious wound, nor in a dangerous part at all, even if it had extended farther than it did - it was about half an inch deep, and near an inch and a half long.
JAMES MORGAN. I am a Policeman. I should think that it must be a quarter or twenty minutes before one o'clock when Shannon met with this accident - I sent a party of men to disperse a party who were kicking up a row, soon after twelve; I was not present when Shannon was wounded - I saw Barrett come out of Anchor and Hope-alley, swearing murder, but what he meant I do not know; Shannon was brought to the station rather before one - I sent him to the doctor's instantly; I cannot say how long before he had been wounded; I had seen the prisoner twenty minutes or half an hour before that; about ten minutes or a quarter of an hour before Shannon was brought in, I saw the prisoner coming out of the passage where he lives, with a knife in his hand - he appeared in a passion, and used the word Murder! with an oath.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the row you saw at the Rose and Crown? A. Very near to it; there were ten or fifteen persons there the first time I saw them - that was twenty minutes or half an hour before Shannon was brought in.
JOHN FLANNINGAN re-examined. As soon as Shannon met with the injury, he was taken home, and in about ten minutes I took him to the station - he was not taken to the doctor's first.
Prisoner's Defence. My father sent me out for half a gallon of beer for supper; a gentleman gave me a shilling to get it - I had a piece of bread and meat in my hand; I met Shannon, Flannigan, and a man named Wilkes at the top of the alley - they said, "Here comes the b - r," and ran after me; I ran along - I came back, and caught hold of a post; they caught hold of me; I got away - they caught me, put me against a door, and threw me down; Flannigan jumped on me, also Shannon and Wilkes - I called Murder! they tried to choke me; I still called Murder! Cotter came - my belly was burst I thought; Maloney came, and after Cotter opened his door, I crept into his house on my hands and face - I got just by the door; Flannigan and Shannon pulled me out by the legs, with my face and hands on the ground, into the middle of the road.
MICHAEL COTTER. I keep a green-grocer's shop. On the night in question I heard a row in the street; I heard the prisoner call out to me for protection before I saw him - I went as far as the door, and saw him; Flannigan came up, caught hold of him by the throat, and threw him down right facing my door; he made an attempt to get up, and Flannigan kicked him on the hip - I went and caught hold of Flannigan; another man came up named Quirk (I think) and made an attempt to kick him, but whether he hit or missed I do not know, and the third man who came up was Shannon; he laid over him, and I having hold of Flannigan, my hat fell off; a man named Maloney, who had been in my shop, came over to me, and lifted Shannon off the prisoner - what the prisoner might have done to Shannon I cannot tell - I said, "Barrett, come into my house;" he said, "My belly is broken, I cannot go" - this was between twelve and one o'clock.
PATRICK MALONEY. On the night this happened I was just outside Cutter's door - Barrett ran up the footpath along the street as hard as he could, and asked Cotter to let him search his place; a man named Flannigan came up, and caught him by the collar; he jostled him down, made a hit at him when he was down, and I believe a kick too - Quirk came up, and made a kick at him while he was down; Shannon came up in a little time; I saved the prisoner as well as I could - I turned back on Shannon, and stopped him from meddling with him; I afterwards found Shannon had received a hurt on his leg - there was no appearance of his being wounded when I first saw him - I assisted in taking him to the doctor's about half an hour after.
COURT. Q. Did you help to take Shannon home? A. I took him on my back from the watch-house to the doctor's - I have no reason to suppose he was hurt till after he followed the prisoner to Cotter's; he was about three perches behind the others - I did not notice that he was lame.
Q. You must have noticed if he was lame at that time? A. I dare say I might - he could hardly walk when at the watch-house.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18310217-210]
Before Mr. Recorder.
636. GUSTAVIUS ADOLPHUS FREDERICK ELLIS was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of November, 1 clock, value 5l., and 1 hat, value 10s., the goods of Richard Kirkman Lane, in his dwelling-house; and CHARLES GAST was indicted for feloniously receiving the said clock, well knowing it to have been stolen.
RICHARD KIRKMAN LANE. I live in Argyle-street, and am a solicitor. I saw my clock safe in my clerk's office about six o'clock in the afternoon of the 27th of November - I went out, and missed it on my return at eight; I saw it at Marlborough-street-office lately, and was satisfied it was mine - I had had it about two years; I believe it to be worth 5l.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What parish is your house in? A. St. James ', Westminster - I do not know the direct way of describing the parish.
MATILDA MOORE. I am servant to Mr. Lane. On the 27th of November, at nearer eight then seven o'clock, there was a knock at the door; I opened it, and saw a tall man, who asked if Mr. Lane was at home; I said he was not, and asked if he would leave his name, or any message, and I would deliver it as soon as he came in - he said there was no occasion for that, if I would allow him to write a letter in the office; I said Certainly - he went into the office; the clock then stood on the mantel-piece; I gave him half a sheet of paper, which he asked for; he then asked if I would allow him to bring a friend in; I said Certainly - he went and let his friend in, and left him on the mat in the passage; the tall man walked into the passage, and I also - I asked his friend to walk into the office, but he made no answer; I asked him again; he walked in - I shut the office door too, and went to acquaint mistress that such persons were there - my mistress had just come down stairs, and was in the back office; I merely told her; she said "Very well" - I shut mistress' door, went into the back hall, and was going down stairs, when mistress told me the gentlemen were gone; I then went into the passage, found the street door open, and looked into the office - they were gone, and the clock also - I saw Ellis at the office on the 7th of this month; he is the man; he is a very tall man - I saw him at the office, and know him to be one of them; I cannot be mistaken about him - I do not recollect the person of the other man who was with him, but am positive it was not Gast; it was a shorter, stouter man, dressed like a baker - I was as certain of Ellis at the office as I am now; I saw the clock there, and was sure of it; I went into the office in a short time in the course of that evening, and found master's hat was taken off the table -I have not seen that since; Ellis had left his own hat behind; I am sure it was his, because the other man's was all over flour.
Cross-examined. Q. How many times were you before the Justice on this business? A. Three times - Ellis was present on the first occasion; I did not state then that I did not know Ellis - I was asked three times, once by a person near, whether I could identify both the prisoners; I said only the tall one - the person said, "I think you can, look at them again;" I said; "No, only the tall one;" it was my master's clerk who said this - I did not hesitate at identifying Ellis.
WILLIAM BALLARD. I am an officer of Marlborough-street. I had been to Gast's house, and went into his bedroom with him, to clean himself, and saw a clock standing on the drawers, on Saturday, the 5th of February - Ellis was at that time in custody on other charges; I said nothing to Gast at this time about the clock - I went with Goddard to Tottenham-court-road, leaving Schofield in care of Gast: when we returned he was in the parlour - he keeps a muffin shop in Hanway-street; I took him up stairs, and requested him to unlock the door, which was locked, and I believe, the key was in it - he unlocked it; I went, laid my hand on the clock, which was on the drawers, and said, "Now, this clock, whose is it?" he said that clock was his wife's before he married her - he repeated that two or three times over; I said, "Well at all events I shall take it, and you must account for it before the Magistrate;" the clock was identified by Mr. Lane and Moore.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was any body present when you had the conversation with Ellis about the clock? A. No - Schofield was down stairs; I saw Gast's wife - he keeps the muffin-shop; it is a small shop, but does a very great business.
BENJAMIN SCHOFIELD. I am an officer. I went to Gast's house, after Ellis was taken, with Ballard and Goddard - I went into the first floor room on the left-hand; it is a bed-room - I saw the clock; I did not see Gast in that room - when Gast was in custody (after Ballard had left the house) he came to me in a side room, where I was waiting to prevent his going out; I then said it was a bad job, he having so respectable a business - I named several things which had been removed from his bed-room by Goddard; I named the clock slightly, but we had not removed that - he merely stated that he knew nothing that was brought into the house, it was all his wife's friends, and his wife's sister had brought the property in; I had not particularly noticed the clock to him then, as I then believed it was his own - after the examination I saw him in the passage of the office, and asked how he accounted for the clock being in his bed-room; I made no threat or promise to him at any time - there was only one clock there; he said that was his wife's, and that she had it before he married her.
JURY. Q. Was any body else in the passage when he said this? A. It was crowded - I am not aware that any body is here.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You questioned him in the passage? A. Yes - any body by must have heard it; Gast's shop has been carried on for a number of years - he pointed out a woman there as his wife; I am informed Ellis is related to him by marriage, and had an apartment in his house - I have frequently seen him go there; Moore gave me a hat on the 10th of February - I have had it ever since.
MATILDA MOORE. The hat has been kept in a cupboard in our house ever since, till I gave it to Schofield-I never saw it in our office till master's was taken; that has not been found - I dusted this clock every morning, and know it.
MR. LANE. This is my clock, I am certain - it cost me eight guineas.
Ellis' Defence. I purchased the clock on a Saturday evening, about eleven o'clock - a young man named Charles Ross, came and said he had a clock to sell; I went with him to look at it - he wanted 5l.; I said I would send my aunt, Mrs. Taylor, to see it, she having been abroker was a judge of such things - she went and bought it; it was brought home to my lodgings, in Hanway-street, and there it has been ever since; at my first examination the first witness could not positively swear to me, which I have witnesses to prove - Mr. Harmer, my solicitor, was in the office at the time; I was yesterday convicted of stealing Mr. Chambers' property, and I actually paid 40l. odd out of my pocket for it.
Gast's Defence. I solemnly declare I never had a conversation with Schofield about the clock - I solemnly declare I never had any conversation with him at all; I married Ellis' mother, a widow with four children, and have worked night and day to bring them up - I put Ellis into business: he failed, and from the affection I had for his mother, I took him into my service, not knowing the company he kept - he could work at the stove while I was in the bakehouse, and what was transacted in the shop while I was in the bakehouse, I was not aware of.
The same written Defence was read as on the prisoners' former trial, page 232.
THOMAS PRICE. I am a hatter, and live in Tottenham-court-road. I was at the office when Moore was examined at Marlborough-street - I attended all the three examinations; I heard her at the first examination asked if she could identify Ellis - she could not; she said so - she was asked by the Magistrate if she could identify any of the prisoners; Ellis was in front of the bar - she looked at them, and had full time to do so; she said she could not identify any of the prisoners - all she know was, that one of the men who came to the house was a dark man, a very dark man; Ellis did not try to screen himself from view, far from it - I was at the second examination; she identified him then - the first examination was on Tuesday; I rather think about the 8th - the second was on Thursday or Friday; she identified him then - he was dressed precisely the same as before; I am a house-keeper.
COURT. Q. How long have you been acquainted with Ellis? A. I was not acquainted with him further than his living at his father-in-law's; I have seen him pass and repass my house, that is all - I went there on Gast's account: a friend of Gast's called on me on Tuesday morning, and I went.
Q. Will you swear Gast was under examination on the 8th? A. Gast was among the prisoners at the bar then; I heard this brought forward about the clock, and attended the next examination; the examination on the 8th was about the clock - on Tuesday Mr. Lane came forward, he said he had lost a clock, and the servant identified it: she swore particularly to Ellis at the second examination, which she did not do on the first - I thought it something peculiar; the second examination was in the Magistrate's private room; I was sitting behind Mr. Harmer, the solicitor, and he was astonished to hear her turn round and say, "That is the man that came to the door, and asked me if Mr. Lane was at home;" Hr. Harmer immediately stood up, and said to Mr. Conant, "That woman before denied knowing any of the prisoners," and she said she did not - I said to Mr. Harmer, "Why, I was present, the Court was cleared for her to look at the prisoners, and she went forward to them and said, 'I cannot identify any of the prisoners, all I know is one was a very dark man.'"
Q. Then she never mentioned a tall man? A. She did not - I am quite sure she never described the man as a tall man at the first examination; she did at the second, and swore positively to Ellis - she said positively at the first examination that she could not identify either of the men; there were fourteen persons at the bar then, and there were two short men who appeared to creep behind the rest, that they might not be observed; but Ellis and Gast remained in front - I have not heard what she has said here; I was not subpoenaed here - I am a hatter: I came at the request of Gast's and Ellis' friends - I told Mr. Harmer I had heard her say that she could not identify either of the prisoners, and he said he hoped I would attend at the Old Bailey; he was by at the time she said this.
MARY MOORE re-examined. I was at the office three times; Ellis was there the first time - that was on Monday, the 7th: I saw him again on Tuesday - on the first examination I was asked to stand on the steps to see them closely; I said I could only see the tall man, and could identify no other but the tall man (the prisoner Ellis) -I was not asked by the Magistrate the first day (Monday), if I could identify them; I saw them again on the second examination (Tuesday) - I said I knew Ellis was the man I let in; I did not express a doubt about him - what I said was not taken down the first day, it was on the second; I am quite sure I spoke positively to him, and at the second examination again I said, "That is the man" - Ellis was present, and saw me.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was not the bar cleared that you might attend to the prisoners? A. It was not cleared, to my knowledge; I went to look at the prisoners - I was asked by one of the officers; the Magistrate did not ask me - Mr. Harmer said I had not identified him at the first examination, but it was very wrong.
Q. Did not Mr. Harmer say something to this effect,"That woman could not, on the first examination, identify the man, and now she has done it?" A. To my knowledge he did not say those words - he said something of the kind, and I contradicted him at the time; I swear that I did identify him on the first occasion - the reason I was asked about him at the second examination was, because I was not asked by any Magistrate on the first day - the Magistrate was sitting, but, I believe, at the time there was something else going on - they were talking about other things; I do no not think the Magistrate could hear it - it was not loud enough for him to hear; the prisoners were before him at the time, but they were talking about other things which were stolen, such as medicine-chests - it was Ballard who asked if I could identify the prisoners - I do not remember Plank asking me; I believe master's clerk asked me first - he asked me three different times over; he said, "There is a short man getting behind, was it not him?" I could identify no one but the tall one - he did not ask me three times to identify the tall one; I identified him at once - I remember where Mr. Harmer stood at the first examination, and my cold was so bad he could not hear me; I am sure I contradicted Mr. Harmer when he said I could not identify him at the first examination - I did it openly - I had seen none of them before, to myknowledge; the man had a coloured handkerchief on - a dark looking one; it was not white - he had a common coat on; I might have seen him for about ten minutes - he took his hat off in the office.
DANIEL LINN. I was formerly in business as a shoe-maker, in Tottenham-court-road - I now live in St. John-street, Clerkenwell, and am out of business. I was examined yesterday to Gast's character - I was not at Marlborough-street on the Monday, but on the Tuesday, and heard Moore asked if she could identify any of the prisoners - the prosecutor's clerk brought the clock up to my righthand, and he was requested to take Moore into the passage that she might see the prisoners brought in, to identify them - they stood at my right-hand, and the answer made was, "No, they warnt them," at the time she went out to see the prisoners walk, I believe Ellis was one - I saw him at the bar; the girl returned and said, "They arn't them that I have seen;" it was then said, "We will have the place cleared; they shall be put to the front, and then you shall go and look;" it was cleared, and the prisoners put to the bar - Ellis and Gast were quite in front, and several women behind; the men were all in front; I think there were five, if not six men - there was nothing to screen Ellis: the officer cleared all the front; she walked up, then walked back, and said, "No, they are not the men;" this was on Tuesday; I was there again on the Friday, at the second examination, when she was asked if she could identify either of them, and she identified the tall one (Ellis) - Mr. Harmer told her she had before said they were not the party; he said so openly - the Magistrate then wanted to mend the evidence, and Mr. Harmer said he had no right so to do.
COURT. Q. What did the girl say to the observation of Mr. Harmer? A. She said she might be mistaken; she did not say she had not mistaken the man, and had said he was the man - she said she might be mistaken; she said three times at the first examination they were not the men, and the clerk stood at her side.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How did you happen to go to the examination? A. Having heard of the circumstance, and Gast being a friend who I highly respected, I went to the examination, as I was going to dine that day in Westbourn-place - I dropped in to render him assistance if he wanted it.
COURT. Q. On your oath, did not the girl, when Mr. Harmer suggested she had not spoken to the man, say that was not true for she had identified him? A. She said, "I might be mistaken" - I positively swear she did not say she had spoken to him - she did not contradict Mr. Harmer- Mr. Harmer rose up, and spoke to her, but I did not hear what she said, more than that she might be mistaken; I will not swear she did not say she had spoken to him, I am sure what I describe took place on Tuesday - I was only there Tuesday and Friday.
Q. Did the Magistrate put the question to her "Did you speak to the man positively the first time?" A. I do not know that I heard a word of that - I was on a chair; the officer said I should break it, and at that moment I got down.
MR. JAMES HARMER. I am solicitor for Gast. I attended at Marlborough-street on two occasions - I do not know when it was; I have only come into Court this moment- I was there at what I understood to be the first examination; Moore was taken by the officer in front of the bar, near to where the prisoner stood - she gave a description as to one being a tall young man; she was asked if she could identify either of the prisoners at the bar - she then came close to the Magistrate's table, and gave a short description of the way the clock was stolen.
Q. Was she able, on being shewn the prisoners, personally, to identify any of them? A. Certainly not - Ellis was at the bar and brought prominently forward; I have heard part of the last witness's examination - I was at the second examination; Moore was examined, but I saw her deposition had been previously reduced to writing - it was put into my hands to read, and seeing it stated her to have identified the prisoner (she had not been sworn to the depositions, and I was surprised to find it reduced to writing - Ballard was giving his testimony as to something Gast had said;) on finding this statement in the deposition, I cannot exactly recollect what I said, but I expressed my astonishment that she should have sworn to the prisoner after she had been shewn him at the bar previously, and could not; Ballard gave an explanation how it occurred.
Q. Did she contradict you flatly at the time, and say she had identified him on the former occasion? A. She said she had spoken to him on a former occasion, but did not say it was in the office; it was after the prisoners had been removed from the office by the officers on their way to the lock-up room, she again had an opportunity of seeing them, and spoke to him - I only heard this from the officers themselves.
JURY. Q. Did the girl in your presence ever say she could not identify the prisoner at the bar? A. Certainly - she said she could not speak to any of the prisoners at the bar, at her first examination.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. At that time Ellis was standing in a prominent situation? A. Yes, and I believe the place was cleared for her.
COURT. Q. When you told the Magistrate she could not identify him on a former occasion, did she contradict you and say she had? A. What passed was from the officer, and not from her - I think the Magistrate was not present when I made the remark; she admitted that she had not spoken to him in the Justice room, but the officer in her presence said, "When we were going out of the office she had a better opportunity, and pointed him out;" this passed at the second examination - the examination was not going on before the Magistrate when I observed to her that she had not spoken to him; I had seen her deposition reduced to writing.
MR. LANE re-examined. When Mr. Harmer observed that Moore had not identified Ellis, Moore, in the most vicious manner, denied the assertion of Mr. Harmer - it is no doubt a mistake of Mr. Harmer's, that he made the observation certainly is true, and she in a quick, galarious way, denied it; I was obliged to say, "Matilda, don't speak so quick."
WILLIAM BALLARD. I asked Moore, at the bar, if she could identify the prisoners - she said she was not certain, but she thought she knew one; I said, "Which one?" and she pointed to Ellis - I said, "Go back again to where I told you to stand before, and make yourself certain; I didnot go out with her, but I saw her afterwards, and she said she was certain.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was this at the examination? A. No - there had not been an examination; they were before the Magistrate, but no depositions were taken - there were fourteen prisoners, and there were several charges made against them.
ELLIS - GUILTY of stealing to the value of 99s. only.
Aged 23. (See page 232.)GAST - NOT GUILTY.

637. CHARLES NICHOLS was indicted for perjury.
