[trialAccount t18310217-212]
Fourth Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
638. JOHN JAMES WOODWARD was indicted for feloniously receiving, of a certain evil-disposed person, 22 yards of woollen cloth, value 2l., the goods of George Meadows and William Benfield, well knowing it to have been stolen.
WILLIAM BENFIELD. I am a tailor, and live in Marylebone-street, Piccadilly, in partnership with George Meadows. On Thursday, the 27th of January, at six o'clock in the evening, I saw this cloth safe in our front shop window, which does not open - I missed it about half-past seven; I am certain it was not sold - it was for garments; we seldom sell cloth - it was Shepherd's plaid for making trousers; there were twenty-three yards in one piece, and nine and three-quarters in another - I saw, at Marlborough-street last Friday, thirteen or fourteen yards of one piece, and the other of nine and three-quarters - one piece has a mark where I had cut out a pattern; I found that mark on it precisely the same, and was confident of it being the same cloth - I know nothing of the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. You have only one partner? A. Only one; I employ nobody in the front shop, but have three apprentices in the workshop - the apprentices and workmen pass through the shop; they leave at eight o'clock - I had gone out about half-past six, and left my people in care of the place; when I came home I found a pane of glass broken or cut, and quite out - it had been cracked the day before by accident; the cloth could be got out there - I discovered it the moment I went into the shop; I was the first to discover it - I found my servants still at work; the shop door cannot be opened outside without a key - I had cut the pattern out myself.
COURT. Q. Did the space in this cloth appear the same size as the pattern cut out? A. Precisely so - I have not got the pattern to compare.
WILLIAM BALLARD. I am an officer. On Thursday, the 3rd of February, about noon, I went with Goddard to No. 9, Fullwood's-rents, and found these two pieces of cloth in a room on the first floor; nobody was in the room - a person who lives in the first floor gave me the key; I left Goddard there. On the following morning, between nine and ten o'clock, I went again with Goddard - the prisoner was not there, and we brought the cloth away; I saw the prisoner that afternoon, at Marlborough-street, in custody - Mr. Benfield did not see the cloth till Friday, the 18th; he brought two patterns, and claimed it - it was two pieces of a different pattern.
Cross-examined. Q. Who gave you the key at the house? A. Mrs. Davidson - she went up with us, and I think unlocked the door.
HENRY GODDARD. I am an officer. On the 3rd of February I went to Fullwood's-rents, with Ballard; Mrs. Davidson opened the room on the first floor - we found the cloth; I waited there till one o'clock in the morning; the prisoner did not come in - I saw him at the office the afternoon of the next day: Mr. Benfield claimed the cloth - on the Tuesday after the Friday, when the prisoner was brought for re-examination, he was sitting in the clerk's-office, but the examination had not commenced; he stated to me that he had bought the cloth of one or two persons who called at his door in Fullwood's-rents, for 1s. a yard, and he could bring a person forward to prove it; he called it stuff for trousers - there was no other stuff for trousers in his room; no owner had been found then - this cloth was not examined about that day.
Cross-examined. Q. Where was he? A. Sitting on a form in the clerk's-office; I mentioned what I had brought from his lodging when he stated this - Schofield was there, but I do not know whether he heard it.
BENJAMIN SCHOFIELD. I apprehended the prisoner at his lodging, in the first floor front room, in Fullwood's-rents - he was making inquiry about this cloth at the time I went in - I heard him told that two officers had taken it away; he said he could account for it, as he bought it at the door for 1s. a yard: I took him to the office, and heard him make the statement to Goddard - there were a few waste pieces of cloth and remnants about his room; he did not mention the name of who he bought it of.
MR. BENFIELD. I am confident this is my cloth; the piece is cut out here - 2s. 2d. a yard is the wholesale price; I never bought any lower - I have not got the pattern.
Cross-examined. Q. I think you said one piece had a pattern cut out? A. Yes; that is between thirteen and fourteen yards - I cannot swear to the other.
COURT. Q. Had you a similar cloth to the other in your possession? A. Yes, but have no particular mark on it - it was precisely the same sort and length, nine yards and three-quarters - the other was longer when stolen.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought the stuff found in my room of a man who hawks linen about; I was called down by a person who lives in the house, to look at it - as to the gentleman swearing to it by the piece being cut out, I have cut patterns out myself.
MR. BENFIELD. The pattern hole is precisely the same at this end as I left it - I do not find that any other pattern has been cut; I cannot say to whom I gave the pattern.
MARIA DAVIDSON. I live at No. 9, Fullwood's-rents - my husband works for Mr. Norris, an upholder, in Holborn. The prisoner lodges at the same house as us, and when out usually leaves his key with me; a man came one day to our door, with a pack at his back, and asked if I would buy the cloth - I said I did not want any thing of the kind; the prisoner being a tailor, I called out, "Mr. Woodward:" he came down - I said, "Here is a man anxiously wishing me to purchase this - can you buy it?" he said to the man, "How much do you want?" the man said 1s. 6d. a yard; I went in doors, and did not troublemy head further; he came in afterwards, and borrowed 2s. 6d. - he took some cloth up with him.
NOT GUILTY. (See page 232.)
[trialAccount t18310217-213]
639. HENRY BURDETT GAST was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 2nd of January, of a certain evil-disposed person. 1 writing-case, value 12s., and 1 patent mauifold-writer, value 1l., the goods of Ralph Wedgwood, well knowing them to have been stolen.
RALPH WEDGWOOD. I live in Rathbone-place, Oxford-street, and am a manufacturer of manifold-writers; I keep an open shop. About the end of November or beginning of December I lost a writing-case and manifold-writer out of my shop; I lost a great many, and cannot state when I had seen them last: I know nothing of the prisoner - I saw these two articles again last Tuesday week at the office; I know them to be my manufacture, and am satisfied they had not been sold.
Cross-examined by MR. HEATON. Q. You have sold many of the same kind, have not you? A. Yes - I should like to see them; I lost twenty or twenty-four that evening, and have seen about eight - part were fitted up ready for sale, and part were not; I never sold them not fitted up.
GEORGE UNDERWOOD. I am a servant to Mr. Nicholls, a pawnbroker, of No. 43, Gray's Inn-lane. I produce a manifold-writer and writing-case which were pledged on the 11th of January - I did not take it in, but was present, and saw the person who pawned it, and am quite certain the prisoner is the man; he has pawned at our shop for a year and a half at least - I cannot be mistaken in him; he pawned it in the name of Thomas Cooper, Gray's Inn-lane, for 12s.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you seen many of these manifold-writers? A. I have not; it appears to be fitted up complete - I did not hear the prisoner give the name; I knew him by the name of Cooper for eighteen months - he always went by that name at our shop; I know his wife, and always took her to be Mrs. Cooper - I do not know her name before marriage.
HENRY DEMPSEY. I am a servant to Mr. Griffith, a pawnbroker of Ossulton-street, Somers'-town. I have a writing-case, pawned on the 14th of January, by the prisoner, in the name of John Cooper, No. 10, Drummond-crescent; we had done business with him before; Mr. Wedgwood saw and claimed it at the first examination.
Cross-examined. Q. He pawned it in the name of Cooper? A. Yes, we always knew him by that name: he was in Mr. Griffith's employ about three years ago I understand - there was no concealment.
JURY. Q. Did he go by the name of Cooper at Mr. Griffith's? A. To the best of my knowledge.
WILLIAM BALLARD. I am an officer. I apprehended the prisoner in Little Drummond-street; he and his wife both stood a few paces from the door - we went into the house; it appeared a chandler's shop - we inquired if Gast lived there; a person pointed him out walking down the street - we went after him, and asked him to come back with us and go into his room; he went readily with us, and said he knew what we were come about, that he expected us; we asked what? he said respecting the business of his brother Gast, who has been tried - we asked if he had been pawning any thing? he said he had pawned a writing-case at Griffith's (we had been there before and saw one) - he was asked if he had pawned any other; he said No; we searched him, but found no duplicate - he said he had pawned things for Ellis, and always gave him the money and duplicate, but denied pawning any other writing-case - I do not know that a manifold-writer was mentloned, except as a writing-case; I found a ticket of a manifold-writer in a room in Tottenham-court-road, said to belong to Ellis, and went to Nicholls' - it is called a writing-case on the duplicate.
Cross-examined. Q. You do not know positively that the room belonged to Ellis? A. No, only it has been sworn so here; it was the attic of Mr. Thompson's house - this conversation was a considerable time after the pawning; he might have forgotten it - he was within five yards of his own door; I did not know him, and do not know that he did me - he did not attempt to escape; his brother and Ellis had been taken up - I found nothing at his lodging.
MR. WEDGWOOD. This is a patent manifold-writer, and I am certain was never sold - this case is mine; it was the cover of a manifold-writer, not fitted up for sale -I would not have sold it in this state; it originally had printed directions on it on both sides, but they appear to have marble paper pasted over them, to disguise it.
The prosecutor was cross-examined at very great length as to his means of identifying the property, and finally pointed out several marks in them, and that the case was stamped "Warranted genuine, R. Wedgwood," with a punch, which never went out of his possession.
NOT GUILTY.

MARY ANN COLEMAN was indicted for stealing 2 shirts, value 4s.; 5 handkerchiefs, value 5s.; 4 pinafores, value 4s.; 2 aprons, value 2s.; 1 jacket, value 4s.; 1 pair of trousers, value 3s.; 1 pair of drawers, value 6d.; 1 cap, value 3d.; 1 collar, value 3d.; 3 pieces of ribbon, value 2s.; 2 bags, value 2s.; 4 pairs of stockings, value 4s.; 4 pairs of gloves, value 3s.; 1 frill, value 6d.; 4 yards of flannel, value 16s.; 5 yards of calico, value 3s.; 1 sheet, value 1s.; 2 books, value 1s.; 1 sponge, value 6d.; 1 scarf, value 5s., and 2 tea-trays, value 1s., the goods of John Bedwell, her master; to which she eaded

642. DANIEL MENSER was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of February, 1 rug, value 1s., the goods of Robert Sharpe; 1 hammer, value 1s.; 1 jigger, value 10d.; 1 pair of boot-irons, value 1s.; 1 fore-part iron, value 4d.; 1 seat iron, value 2d.; 1 stick, value 1 1/2d.; 2 awls, value 1d., and 1 shirt, value 6d., the goods of John Bridges ; to which he pleaded

643. GEORGE HILLIARD was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of February, 1 pair of scales, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Isabella Jones, widow.
JAMES KENNERLY. On the evening of the 1st of February I was in High-street, St. Giles', and saw the prisoner, in company with another person - I followed the prisoner, who was going my way; I met Betraun - we followed the prisoner and the other on to Holborn, and from there on to Great Warner-street, Clerkenwell; I there saw his companion push open the door of the shop No. 9 - he came out again immediately, and joined the prisoner, who was close by him when he went in - they then both ran off. I followed them, and laid hold of both of them; but the other got away; I kept the prisoner till Betraun came up.
ANGELIOUS BETRAUN. On the evening of the 1st of February I joined Kennerly in High-street, St. Giles' - we followed them to Great Warner-street; I received the prisoner from Kennerly, and in his jacket pocket I found these scales.
ANN HUNT. I am the daughter of Isabella Jones, a widow, who keeps a shop at No. 9, Great Warner-street. These scales are hers - I used them about three minutes before they were missed.
Prisoner's Defence. The other boy took them, and I did not know of it - he saw them coming, and tried to shove them into my pocket.
JAMES KENNERLY. He said so at the time, but I did not see it.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18310217-218]
644. JOSIAH RANDALL was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of January, 1 saw, value 8s., the goods of Emanuel Coward.
EMANUEL COWARD. I am a carpenter, and live in Gray's Inn-lane. I was at work at Messrs. Child and Vickers, the distillers, in Stone-street, Borough. On the 31st of January I left my tools in a basket in the stable there - I went again about a quarter before one o'clock, and missed a saw out of my tools; I made inquiry, but could not find it.
THOMAS COWARD. I am the prosecutor's father - I have known the prisoner twenty years or nearly so. In consequence of information, I went after him, and found him near the Crown, in Holywell-lane - I told him he had been taking a saw from Child and Vicker's distillery - he denied it; I said, "You have pawned it, give me the duplicate;" I went with him to his lodging, and after some conversation, he gave me the duplicate.
WILLIAM CLARK. I am shopman to Mr. Attenborough, a pawnbroker, in Crown-street. On the 31st of January, about dinner time, this saw was pawned by the prisoner; I lent him 2s. 6d. on it, and gave this duplicate.
THOMAS WHITTLE. I am a Police-constable. I took the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoner pleaded poverty.
GUILTY. Aged 46. - Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18310217-219]
645. JOHN HART was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of February, 1 pair of boots, value 8s. 6d., the goods of Ephraim Wigginton.
MARY WIGGINTON. I am the wife of Ephraim Wigginton - we live in Old-street, and keep a shoe warehouse. On the 12th of February I saw the prisoner at our shop; he looked at a pair of boots and a pair of shoes also - he desired me to put up the shoes in the window; while I was doing that he ran out - I looked, and missed the boots immediately; in consequence of information I went on the following morning to Ironmonger-row, where I saw him at work; I knew him to be the man, and I asked him for the money for the boots which he took away on the Saturday; he said he was not looking at boots, he was looking at shoes - I said he had taken away the boots which he had on: I knew they were the boots which had been in my window - he said he had bought them in Gravel-lane; he first said at a house, and then he said of a man - I got an officer, and he was taken - I know the boots by a mark inside them.
Prisoner. She said I wore fustian trousers. Witness. No, it was dark - I said I was not sure whether he had the same trousers on then which he had before.
WILLIAM BELSHAM. I am in the prosecutor's service. I know these boots - I had been in the habit of cleaning them; I saw the prisoner in the shop when I was sent out, and when I came back he was gone - I saw him when he was taken, and knew him to be the same man.
HENRY PERRY. I am a Police-constable. The prisoner was brought to our station with these boots on his feet - he said he had bought them of a man of the name of Callaghan, who worked at Curtis' bacon yard over the water, for 8s. 6d. on the Saturday evening.
JOHN CALLAGHAN. I am a labourer at Mr. Curtis' bacon yard, in Holland-street. I never sold a pair of boots to the prisoner, or lent him or gave him any - I have known him some time; he brought a pair of boots to my house on the Saturday before he was apprehended, and said he bought them in Ironmonger-row - I do not know whether these are them.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not buy them nor steal them - I went in to buy a pair of boots; I was drunk, and did not know what I did.
GUILTY. Aged 25. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18310217-220]
646. JOSEPH MOORE and BENJAMIN JENNINGS were indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of January, 18 bushels of soot, value 15s., the goods of Thomas Spearing.
THOMAS SPEARING. I live in Broomyard-alley, Longalley. I was called out of my bed between three and four o'clock - I went to my shed, found the door open, and the lock and staple had been drawn; I missed a quantity of soot which had been loose about the shed - I stated it as ten sacks at first, but that was not correct; I suppose six sacks were gone - I went to No. 10, Cock-alley, and found the prisoners there; some soot was on the floor of the room they were in - I called for a basin of water to try whether it was my property or not, and it was; because there was saw dust mixed with it as there had been with the soot at my shed - we cannot help taking up the ashes of whatever there is mixed with the soot, and this was saw dust of mahogany - I compared it with that at my shed, and found it was the same - it is very common in the trade to mix saw-dust with soot.
ROGER RAGAN. I am a Police-constable. On the 22nd of January I was in Long-alley at two o'clock in the morning, and saw the two prisoners coming along with two sacks of soot; I questioned them as to where they got it - they said where they had been at work, at the London tavern; I said, "I can't believe you - I think you would not be at the London tavern at this time in the morning;" they said if I was not satisfied I might go with them, and I went to No. 10, Cock-alley, where they shot the soot out on the floor of an empty room - I asked if they occupied that room; they said they did, but one of the witnesses came down, and said, "Good God! what brought you here at this time in the morning?" I asked if they lived there, and she said No- they then said they had taken it of the landlady the day before; I gave an alarm, and Stephen Renew came up - I left him in charge of the property, and took Moore to the station-house - in going along he told me it was his own property; I could not see Jennings at the time I took Moore, for he had got up the chimney - I brought Moore back from the station-house to the room, and then I found Jennings there - that was half an hour afterwards, or rather more.
STEPHEN RENEW. I live at No. 15, Great George-street, Mile-end, and am a watchman. Ragan called me into the house in Cock-alley; I saw Moore there, but I did not then see Jennings: after Moore had been taken to the station I saw some mortar fall down the chimney - a boy named Onion came in; I told him to look up the chimney - Jennings then came down; Moore was afterwards brought back, and then both the prisoners were taken.
Jennings. I was not up the chimney - I sat on the stove.
SAMUEL ONION. I went in, and looked up the chimney - I saw Jennings there; I had known him before - he went by the name of Ben Matthews; I said, "I know who it is - it is Ben Matthews - you may as well come down;" he made no answer: I found some shoes in the yard where Spearing's shed is, but I do not know of either of the prisoners wearing them.
ROSANNA KEMP. I live at No. 10, Cock-alley, Nortonfalgate. I heard a noise about two o'clock in the morning - I went down, and saw the officer, the two prisoners, and a quantity of soot in a room; I had seen them there, but they had not lived there.
JOHN BURNESS. I live at No. 2, Cock-alley. About half-past three o'clock that morning I went to No. 10 - I saw Renew there, and a quantity of soot; the officer afterwards brought Moore in; I got a lantern, and tracked some soot about a hundred yards from No. 10, Cock-alley, to about forty yards from the prosecutor's shed - the track ceased in Long-alley; I suppose the sacks had been opened there - there was no track at the door of Spearing's shed; the track ceased very near Smith's-buildings - I traced it through the passage of a double house, No. 14, both the doors of which were open; I saw the staple and lock were off the shed, and there was nothing to prevent a person then from going in - I found a piece of tin, which appeared to have been used as a shovel.
ROGER RAGAN. I walked through the double house; when I first saw the prisoners they were in Long-alley, and appeared to be coming in a direction from the shed, which Spearing says had been broken - I think they were sixty yards off it.
HENRY WRIGHT. I am foreman to Thomas Spearing. I compared the soot found at Cock-alley with that on my master's premises; I believe it had formed a part of it -I had mixed it for my master; I saw at Cock-alley about seven pecks shot out, and one sack which had not been shot out - it was worth 7 1/2d. a bushel; my master did not miss any sack - the soot might have been mixed by other persons - I did not find near as much soot as was missing from the shed; I suppose there were thirty sacks of soot in the shed.
Jennings. We were doing work for ourselves, and we had been out to try to get a little soot - we put it down against the wall by the London tavern; that was what I told the officer, and that it belonged to me.
JURY to ROGER RAGAN. Q. Did you say you saw two sacks? A. Yes - I went with them to the room; I then took a short turn round my beat and returned - part of the soot had been conveyed away; the sacks appeared full when I first got up to them.
COURT. Q. How long were you absent? A. About five minutes - the prisoners went out, and said they were going to the London tavern for more.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18310217-221]
647. JOSEPH MOORE was again indicted for stealing, on the 15th of January, 36 bushels of soot, value 30s., the goods of Susannah Collins.
SUSANNAH COLLINS. I live at No. 1, Sharp's-alley, and am a widow. I have a shed near Grey Eagle-street, which was secured by an iron padlock - I went there about eight o'clock in the morning, on the 15th of January, found it had been broken open, and missed about twelve sacks of soot; I found a handkerchief, which I gave to the officer- I had seen my shed safe on the night before; the soot I missed was worth 30s., it is 2s. 6d. a sack - I traced some sooty foot-marks, from Grey Eagle-street to the top of Pearl-street, opposite Fleur-de-lis-court; it was a damp morning, and that was the reason I suppose.
SAMUEL ONION. I am an apprentice to Mr. Peter Savage, Moore's-alley, Norton-falgate - he is a chimney-sweep. I have known the prisoner these five years; he was in the habit of bringing soot to my master, about six months before he was taken - when he was engaged in shooting the soot, I used to hold his cap, and I used to see a handkerchief in it; I observed it particularly - I noticed a tear in the corner, and three letters on it; I have had it twenty times in my hand - there was a cook-shop at the corner of Moore's-alley, which he used to frequent, and I have seen him use the handkerchief there; I remember on one occasion, we were standing at the corner of Cock-alley, and he had it in his hand - we used to call him by the nickname of Buss, and I said, "Buss, let me look at your handkerchief;" I looked at it, and said, "It is torn at the corner;" he said, "I know it is;" there were three letters on it in a straight line - this is the handkerchief, here is the tear; here are three letters in a straight line, and one below; I had not noticed the one below at that time.
Prisoner. Q. When did I show you the handkerchief? A. I cannot rightly tell; it was about three days before this happened.
JURY. Q. How long before he was apprehended had you seen him shoot any soot? A. About six months - he was not in my master's employ, but he used to buy soot of the prisoner.
JOHN BURNESS. I am a private watchman. I was at the corner of Fleur-de-lis-court, on the morning of the 15th of January, and saw two sweeps - one had a sack of soot on his back, and the other was following him; they came as if from Grey Eagle-street, which is about one hundred yards from there - I saw them return twice with empty sacks; I saw them afterwards in Blossom-street - Barnard was following them.
BENJAMIN BARNARD. I saw two men carrying soot - I believe the prisoner was one, but cannot swear it.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18310217-222]
648. BENJAMIN JENNINGS was again indicted for stealing, on the 6th of December, 1 watch, value 2l., the goods of John Rose.
ELIZABETH ROSE. I am the wife of John Rose - we live in Walter-place, School-house-lane, Ratcliff; my husband is a waterman. On the 6th of December, I called in the prisoner to sweep the kitchen chimney, about ten o'clock in the morning - my husband's watch was hanging over the chimney; when the prisoner had got the machine about half-way up, he told me to take down the watch, which I did - I put it into a basin on the dresser, and covered it over with a little silk bag; I went up stairs to get some money, and left my little girl in the room, but she went out to see if the machine was up the chimney - I was away about a minute and a half, and when I came down she was out in the yard; the prisoner soon finished the chimney - he made some difficulty about putting the soot into the bag, and I asked if I should hold it; he said No - I paid him 4d., and he went away; I missed the watch in about ten minutes - no one had been in the house but him, from the time I had put it into the basin; I received information, and went to White's-yard, Rosemary-lane, saw the prisoner, caught him by the jacket, and said,"Where is the watch you took from Walter-place, Schoolhouse-yard, where you swept a chimney this morning?" he said, "You had better come and see;" he struggled, got from me, and ran as hard as he could - I value the watch at two guineas; it cost me four - it was as good for me as when I bought it; my daughter is twelve years old, but she is very ill - I am certain no one was in the house but her and the prisoner.
Prisoner. Before she saw me she wanted to swear to another man of the name of Fossett. Witness. No - a Policeman took another person, but I said that was not the man. I was informed he either resorted to, or lived in White's-yard - I went there, he came out of a passage, and I caught him.
DAVID FOSSETT. I live in Essex-street, Commercial-road. I have known the prisoner a great while - two Policemen came, took me to the office, and the prosecutrix was going to swear to me, till I took her under a gaslight, and said, "My good woman, what are you going to swear to?" I took off my cap, and then she said I was not the man - if I had not spoken to her, she would have sworn to me, and I should have been where the prisoner is now; I never knew any harm of the prisoner.
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18310217-223]
649. SUSANNAH MANLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of November, 1 sheet, value 2s., and 1 blanket, value 7s., the goods of Daniel Kemmish.
DANIEL KEMMISH. I am a bellows-maker, and live in Tent-street, St. George's; the prisoner's husband keeps the house, and she lives there. About the latter end of November my wife missed a blanket off the bed; we had a box in the room, from which the sheet was taken - I do not know when I had seen that; I cannot say that I ever saw it in the box. On the 25th of January the prisoner and her husband quarrelled, and at her husband's instigation I had her taken up - I have since seen a sheet in the possession of Pool, which I know had been on our bed; the prisoner's husband gave me a duplicate, in her presence, which I gave to the officer.
DANIEL KEMMISH, JUN. I am thirteen years old. I was left at home by my mother one morning, two or three months ago; the prisoner came into the room we live in, and asked me to go to my aunt's to know if she had mixed the bread up - I went, and as I was coming back I met my little brother, whom she had sent out; she then sent me out again for some flour, and said the bread was too wet - my father was out, and my mother was at Tottenham.
Prisoner. That was at a different time altogether; it was a fortnight afterwards - I sent him for the flour as his mother had left the bread running all over the place.
WILLIAM POOL. I am in the employ of a pawnbroker, in Ratcliff-highway. I have a sheet, pawned on the 30th of November, by the prisoner, in the name of Smith - she was in the habit of pawning at our shop by the name of Smith, New Gravel-lane, which is fifty or sixty yards from Tent-street.
JAMES KENNEDY. I was called into the house in Tent-street, on the 25th of January; I took the prisoner there - her husband was there, and gave Kemmiah a duplicate; he said that was his property, and the prisoner ought to be punished - Kemmish gave me the duplicate, and I took the prisoner to the watch-house; she there said that Kemmish's wife gave the sheet to her to pawn.
LOUISA KEMMISH. I know this sheet is my husband's; it was kept in a box under the bed - I missed it on the night I came home from Tottenham; I do not know the day - it was in November or December, I think in December; I had never given the prisoner, or any one else, permission to pawn it; she and her husband live on very bad terms.
COURT. Q. Now, take care what you are saying, it affects the prisoner very much - are you quite sure you had not given her the sheet to pawn? A. Yes; we did owe some rent at that time - her husband has not excused it; we still owe about 12s. 6d.; we have two rooms there - we have never found the blanket; 1s. 6d. was lent on the sheet - when I went into the room to get it out of the box it was gone; I looked well about, and then I went into one of their rooms - I said, "Mrs. Manley, I have lost a sheet;" and she hooted out of another room,"Oh, you will find it again" - but I think she was in liquor; her husband then said, "That is all you get for going into their room when she was ill."
JURY. Q. Were you in the habit of drinking with her? A. No.
COURT. Q. Now, are you quite certain about the sheet? A. I am quite certain, if it were the last word I had to speak, that I never gave her the sheet to pawn - I frequently heard her husband say he could have her transported.
Prisoner. I went to her one morning, and said I had an article in pawn, and I did not know how to get it out; she said, "I have nothing but a sheet;" she brought it to me wrapped up in her shawl, that the children might not see it. Witness. She never pawned any thing of mine, with my knowledge, but a footman, and then I sent my boy with her.
COURT. Q. Do you mean, upon the solemn oath you have taken, to say that you did not deliver that sheet to her to pawn? A. No, my Lord, I did not, if it were the last word I had to speak; that is the truth, the whole truth, and nothing but the truth - her husband came to the window while they were quarrelling, and said, "Mr. Kemmish, if you will fetch an officer I will tell you where your property is."
JURY to DANIEL KEMMISH, SEN. Q. Where did the prisoner's husband produce the duplicate from? A. I was at work at the window and he said to me, "Mr. Kemmish fetch an officer, and I will discover to you your stolen property" I fetched an officer, and when I took him in the yard her husband gave me the duplicate.
COURT. Q. Do you mean that when you fetched the officer you did not know who was the thief? A. No, my Lord; I made all the haste I could, for fear he should alter his mind - he was in a passion with his wife, and I was afraid he would not give me up the duplicate - I did not know he had a duplicate, but he said he would make a discovery to me, and I judged he had one - I knew my wife had sent the footman to pawn; it was pawned to get something for my family to eat - the prisoner offered her service to take it; I never heard from my wife that she gave her the sheet to pawn; I do not believe she did, for she was at Tottenham when it was stolen.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to ask her for rent - she said she had no money; I said I wanted to fetch an article out, and she lent me this sheet - my husband used me ill, and he persuaded her to prosecute me; she had lent me 2s., and we quarrelled about it.
LOUISA KEMMISH. If I was dying at this moment I know nothing of the sheet being pawned, nor did I lend it to her.
- WILKINSON. I live at No. 15, Wood's-buildings, Whitechapel-road. I met the prosecutrix yesterday at the public-house over the way, and she told me she had lent the prisoner the sheet, but was afraid to tell her husband of it - she was so drunk she was obliged to be brought out of the house; I was there six hours with her - she said the money was spent in liquor between them.
MARY MURRAY. I live in Cromer-street. I heard the prosecutrix say she lent the sheet to the prisoner, but she dare not tell her husband, or he would turn her out of doors - she was not in liquor then, but got very tipsy afterwards.
DANIEL KEMMISH. My wife was not tipsy yesterday; she was not out of my sight at all.
THOMAS MURRAY. I was waiting at the public-house with my wife yesterday to give the prisoner a character -Mrs. Kemmish came there, and had two glasses of rum and a good many glasses of ale, till she could not stand.
JAMES KENNEDY. I am the Policeman in this case. Pool came to me yesterday, and told me the prosecutrix was getting tipsy - I told her husband he had better take her away, or he would lose all his expences.
WILLIAM POOL. She was so drunk yesterday she could hardly stand, and she was so at the office.
DANIEL KEMMISH. My wife was not tipsy.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18310217-224]
650. WILLIAM MARTIN was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of February, 1 handkerchief, value 1s. 6d., the goods of a person unknown, from his person.
RICHARD THOMAS, I live in Titchfield-street, and am an undertaker. On the afternoon of the 13th of February I was crossing from Portland-place - I saw the prisoner behind a gentleman, and he took a handkerchief from his pocket; a little girl told the gentleman of it - he was stopped, and the handkerchief was thrown down an area; I am quite sure I saw it in the prisoner's hand, and he ran away with it - this is it.
Prisoner. I was above one hundred yards from the place when I was taken; I had not been out of my own yard ten minutes Witness. I am quite sure I saw him put his hand in the gentleman's pocket, and I ran after him - he ran down Riding-house-lane, and was taken in Titchfield-street - I was not four yards from him when he took it.
JURY. Q. Did you see him throw it down the area? -A. No, a neighbour did.
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming down Great Titchfield-street, and a gentleman came and took me to an area, where there was a handkerchief - the gentleman would have let me go, but this witness came up and said he saw me pick the pocket.
JOHN COLLISON. I took the prisoner in Foley-street; a gentleman had him by the collar - he was charged with picking his pocket of this handkerchief, which he delivered to me.
RICHARD THOMAS. This is the handkerchief - I saw it picked up from the area by an opposite neighbour, and the gentleman took it.
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18310217-225]
651. GEORGE PARSONS PHELPS, DANIEL CLARKE, and WILLIAM NELSON, were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of January, 1 pair of trousers, value 14s., the goods of Thomas Evans; and GEORGE FLANNER was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
JANE EVANS. I am the wife of Thomas Evans - we live in Pelham-street, Spitalfields. On the 14th of January I put a pair of trousers out in our yard, at half-past two o'clock - I went out at four, and saw them safe; there is a wall round our yard, and on the other side of it is Mr. Sykes' timber-yard; I missed the trousers at a quarter-past four.
ALFRED MILES. I am the son of Ann Miles - we live in Hunt-street, Mile-end New-town. On the 14th of January, about four o'clock, I was in Mr. Sykes' timber-yard, playing with some boys; the three boy s at the barcame into that yard, and said they would mill us if we did not go out - the boys who were at play with me went out, but I staid: the prisoners were then going to rub dirt in my eyes, but I ran through a house - I then returned to the yard, and saw Phelps and Clarke, but not Nelson - I saw Phelps jump off the wall of Mr. Evans' yard, with something under his jacket; Clarke was standing about six inches from the wall - his face was to the wall, and he appeared to move about; they then went out of the gate together, and went up Montague-street.
THOMAS EVANS. I went, in consequence of some information, to No. 4, George-street, Whitechapel, with the officer; I saw Flanner there - he asked if I had lost any thing; I said Yes, a pair of trousers, on the 14th, between four and five o'clock in the afternoon - he gave a duplicate from his hand to the officer; we then went to the pawnbroker with Flanner, where these trousers were produced, which are those I lost.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHILL. Q. Is that all that Flanner said? A. Yes, all he said to me - the officer had been in some time before me; I went to the office with the officer and Flanner - I am quite certain these are my trousers; here is a mark where they are broken out behind, when I went larking at a public-house the first night I had them, and here is a hole at the bottom of the legs.
JAMES HOLEHOUSE. I am shopman to a pawnbroker in Whitechapel. I have a pair of trousers, pawned at our shop by Flanner, on the 14th of January, about six o'clock in the evening, for 5s. - I gave him this duplicate, and I have the counterpart of it.
Cross-examined. Q. How far is your house from George-street? A. About three hundred yards - Flanner had before pawned things with us - he gave his right name.
ZACHARIAH BAKER. I am a Police-officer. I apprehended Nelson, in consequence of some information, in his mother's house - his mother let me in; when I saw him he said, "I did not take the trousers, it was Phelps, and they were sold at Flanner's, in George-yard, for 4s. 6d.;" he said the money had been divided between three of them, and he had been to the theatre in the evening; I took Phelps and Clarke to the station, and on the following morning I went with the prosecutor to Flanner's, in George-street; I rapped several times at the door - Flanner put his head out, and said, "Who is there?" I said, "A friend;" he let me in, and the prosecutor staid in the street for a minute or two; I asked Flanner where the trousers were which he had bought of the three boys; he hesitated, and said they were not in the house - I said, "Have you pawned them?" he said Yes; I said, "Give me the duplicate, and I will get them" - the prosecutor came in while he had the duplicate in his hand; we went to the pawnbroker's - Flanner said when he gave me the duplicate, "There is no occasion for me to come - I have not the money to get them out;" I said, "I can get them without money;" he did not state where he got them.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he at any time tell you where he got them? A. No, not till he got to Worship-street - he was at first admitted as a witness; I was at his door three or four minutes before I got in - I was in plain clothes; he could not tell who I was - there was time for him to have destroyed the duplicate; the prosecutor was at the door two or three minutes after I got in - I have seen Flanner before; he is married, but I do not know his family.
Nelson. I did not tell the Policeman what he has stated - two women at No. 9, Rose-lane, told him that a pair of trousers were brought in there, and Flanner had bought the duplicate. Witness. No, I did not hear that till Flanner stated it at Worship-street.
Phelps. I was not there when they were bargaining for these trousers - I was going along, and a woman called me over, as my father was there intoxicated; I took him home, and did not see Nelson or Clarke till half an hour afterwards, in Rose-lane.
Nelson. The Policeman called me aside, and said, "I will give you 10s. if you will tell me where Flanner lives;" I said, "I don't know, only a woman said he lives in George-street."
ZACHARIAH BAKER. I said nothing of the kind.
Flanner's Defence. I was called on between five and six o'clock in the evening, and a woman asked me to buy a pair of blue trousers; I saw the trousers on the bed - I said, "I don't want them - I have no money;" she then said I might go and pawn them - I said, "I don't like such a thing - I don't know whose they are," but I took and pawned them - I gave her the money: one of the boys went with me - she kept the 4s. 6d., and said the boy might have the halfpence himself - the trousers were not in my house; I have a family of ten motherless children to support.
Flanner received a good character.
PHELPS - GUILTY. Aged 13.
Transported for Seven Years.
CLARKE - GUILTY. Aged 12.
Confined Seven Days, and Whipped.
FLANNER - GUILTY. Aged 44.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined One Month.
NELSON - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18310217-226]
652. CATHERINE RAGAN was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of February, 1 half-sovereign, the money of Thomas Kirby.
THOMAS KIRBY. I am a seaman, and lodge at the Dundee Arms, in Wapping. On the evening of the 16th of February I saw the prisoner, who asked if I would purchase any oysters - she said she had a large family, and I said I had no objection to buy her some beef-steaks for her family; we went to a shop - I ordered some for her, and was going to pay for them; I had a half-sovereign and some silver in my watch-fob, in a bit of paper - I opened the paper, and some of it fell; the butcher said I had dropped something, and he was confident by the chink of it that it was gold - I begged for a light; the prisoner stooped down, and took up a sixpence, which she was very willing to give me, but she was seen to take up the half-sovereign, and I gave charge of her.
Prisoner. When I first met him in a public-house he made me take a couple of glasses of liquor, which I am not accustomed to; I do not know whether I took it up or not. Witness. I had not been in any public-house with her - she complained of poverty, and said she had nine children; I had made a pretty good voyage, and thought I would relieve her.
WILLIAM SAUNDERS. I am a butcher, and live in Old Gravel-lane. The prisoner and the prosecutor came to my shop on the 16th of February, to buy a bit of beef - he was going to pay, and some money fell down on the pavement; the prisoner stooped, and appeared to me to pick up something, and put into her pocket - she stooped again, and picked up a sixpence, which she gave the prosecutor; I thought, by the sound, that a half-sovereign had dropped.
JOHN EASTERBROOK. I am a Police-serjeant. I was in the inner-room at the watch-house while the prisoner was in the outer room; I went into the outer room, and as I went in the prisoner moved - I heard something fall; I looked, and saw a half-sovereign on the floor, by her feet; I said, "I thought you said you had not had it;" she said she had never seen it, nor touched it - she appeared sober before, but appeared very drunk when I found it.
WILLIAM SAUNDERS. She appeared sober when I saw her.
Prisoner's Defence. He made me drink four glasses of liquor, and put a pipe into my mouth to smoke, which was found in my pocket - he said he would be better to me than my husband.
THOMAS KIRBY. It is no such thing - I was not half an hour with her; I was in no house with her, and did not give her any thing to drink.
JURY. Q. Did you ask where she lived? A. No, but she said she had a family - I never saw her before.
RICHARD PARKHURST. I am a Police-constable. I took the prisoner to the watch-house - I found 3s. 6d. on her; I afterwards heard the half-sovereign fall from her clothes, and saw it picked up.
GUILTY. Aged 32. - Confined One Week.
[trialAccount t18310217-227]
653. JOSEPH SOUNDY was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of January, 1 pair of sheets, value 10s., the goods of Charles Thomas.
ELIZABETH THOMAS. I am the wife of Charles Thomas - we live in Castle-street, Oxford-market. The prisoner occupied the back room on our second floor, which was let furnished - he was taken into custody on the 22nd of January, and the Policeman came and asked if my room was safe; I then examined, and missed a pair of sheets, which had been on his bed, and had been let with the room.
JOHN COOKE. I am shopman to a pawnbroker in Wardour-street. On the 4th of January these sheets were pawned at our shop, and I verily believe the prisoner to be the person who pawned them; they are in the name of William Richardson, lodger, No. 6, Wardour-street, for 7s.
Prisoner. That direction is of your own putting - Witness. No, I asked the question, as I always do.
WILLIAM SIBLEY. I am a Police-constable. I took the prisoner, and found this duplicate on him.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I did not do it with any intention to defraud, but to supply a moment of need.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 42. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18310217-228]
654. CHARLOTTE SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of February, 2 sovereigns and 1 sixpence, the monies of John Wilson, from his person.
JOHN WILSON. I am a shipwright, and live in John-street, Limehouse-fields. I met the prisoner near Finsbury-square, on the 10th of February; she accosted me, and asked me if I would give her any gin; I gave her some at two different houses; we then went to a house in Winfield-street - we went into a bed-room; I staid there more than half an hour - I had two sovereigns and one sixpence in my pocket, which I know were safe ten minutes before I went into the room; I observed her put her hand into my right-hand pocket, and I gave her in charge - I was rather fresh with liquor.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Where did you meet her? A. At the corner of Finsbury-square - we went to a public-house - I had a glass of gin at two houses, and gave her the same; we then went on towards Spitalfield's church, and she proffered to show me the way - I do not remember what time it was; I do not remember telling her it was too late for me to go home - I do not know who proposed my going with her; she seduced me - I do not remember seeing the landlady; I did not pay for the room, nor give the prisoner any thing, nor send out for more liquor, nor lay down on the bed - I had requested her to let me go home before I went in, but I was rather in liquor and went with her; I kept all my clothes on all the time - I do not remember a sovereign being on the floor, and our having a dispute about it - I had not seen any one else in the house, nor made any bargain; I was standing in the room when the Policeman came in - I had put the candle out with snuffing it, about three minutes before the officer came in - I do not know whether she had laid down; I am married.
JOHN ROBINSON. I am a Police-constable. I was on duty near Petticoat-lane - I heard a call for Police, and went to Gun-yard, Petticoat-lane; I found the prosecutor and the prisoner together in the dark, in a lower room - the prosecutor was a good deal the worse for liquor, and the prisoner was worse than him; I took the prisoner - I have one sovereign which I received from Mrs. Francis.
Cross-examined. Q. You say the prisoner was tipsy? A. Yes - they were both so tipsy they hardly knew what they were about; the prisoner was the worst - the landlady brought me a light, but she did not come into the room; the prisoner was so tipsy I had to carry her to the station-house nearly - she behaved very riotous, and bit and struck me; the bed appeared to me as if some one had been lying on it - the prisoner's gown was nearly off, but the prosecutor had his clothes and his hat on - it was between twelve and one o'clock.
SUSANNAH FRANCIS. I am the wife of Edward Francis, a Police-constable; I live at the station-house, in Church-street, Spitalfields. I saw the prisoner the next morning, and searched her - I told her to take her stays off; she put her hand into her bosom, took out two sovereigns, put one of them in her mouth, made a bolt, and swallowed it - she then put the other into her mouth, but I seized the back of her neck and made her put that out; I gave it to the officer.
Cross-examined. Q. She had recovered her senses then? A. Yes.
Prisoner's Defence. It is false - I dropped one sovereign, but I did not swallow one; I picked up the sovereign, but did not know it was the prosecutor's.
GUILTY. Aged 25. - Transported for 14 Years.
[trialAccount t18310217-229]
655. JOHN TOWERS was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of February, 1 waistcoat, value 1s. 6d., the goods of James Kevill.
JAMES KEVILL. I live in Lucas-street; the prisoner was servant to Captain Pearson, and lived in the same house. I saw a waistcoat at the Police-office, which was mine; I had not missed it - it had been in a trunk in my room.
DAVID MOSES. I am in the employ of my brother, who keeps a slopseller's shop, in High-street, Shadwell. On the 16th of February the prisoner came, and brought a waistcoat in his hat, which he asked me to buy - I asked whose it was? he said his own - he asked 2s. for it, and I gave him 1s. 6d.; I gave the same waistcoat to the prosecutor.
HENRY LEE. I am a Police-constable. On the 16th of February I took the prisoner, in the Back-road, St, George's in the East - I said, "Don't you live at Mr. Pearson's?" he said Yes - I said, you must go with me; he said he had taken the waistcoat.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I lived with Captain Pearson some months - I was sick, and I took the waistcoat to get some medicine from the hospital; I came from Spanish-town, Jamaica.
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Recommended to Mercy. - Fined 1s., and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18310217-230]
656. JOHN WATTS was indicted for embezzlement.
THOMAS DAFFURN. The prisoner was in my employ to do odd jobs, for which he receives 7s. a week from each coachman. On the Wednesday before the 5th of February he was sent out with a cart - he was to remove some furniture; one of my men sent him to the job - I do not know what passed between the man and him; I saw the prisoner when he came back, and received 3s. 6d. from him - he said that was all he could get, but the job came to 5s. 3d.
WILLIAM DAIRY. On the 2nd of February I told my wife to pay the prisoner 5s. 3d. for three hours and a half, at 1s. 6d. an hour - it was for moving some goods in Mr. Daffurn's van: the prisoner had the management of it -I saw the money paid to him.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Have you a person in your employ named Charles Franklin? A. No-I had sent a person to the yard for the van, but he is not here; I do not know who he delivered the message to.
MR. DAFFURN re-examined. Q. Had the prisoner any permission to attend with the cart? A. Certainly, if a job came in; I had not that day given him authority to go with it - I was not at home; he had a general authority to go with it, and receive money - he had been a regular servant at one time, and has worked there two or three years.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you a person in your employ named Charles Franklin? A. Yes, but he is not here; it would not have required any authority for the prisoner to go with the van.
Prisoner's Defence. Mr. Hancock came to me with a message from Mr. Dairy to remove some goods; I went to do the job on my own account - I met Mr. Franklin, and asked him for a van, which he said I could have; I went, received 5s. of Mr. Dairy, and there was a man with the van who had 3d. - I went home, and paid my master 3s. 6d. for two hours and three-quarters, at 1s. 3d. an hour - I kept the other 1s. 6d. for my trouble.
MR. DAFFURN. If he had asked me to let him do it, I, perhaps, should not have refused him, but he ought to have asked me - he would have received 6d. an hour for the job.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18310217-231]
657. LEONARD JAMES MITCHELL and ELIZABETH MITCHELL were indicted for stealing, on the 30th of October, 2 pillows, value 3s.; 1 bolster, value 3s.; 1 piece of patchwork, value 2s., and 1 looking-glass, value 1s., the goods of John Crawford.
JOHN CRAWFORD. The prisoners were lodgers of mine, and passed as brother and sister. I missed this property on the 17th of January - the prisoners had been taken into custody on another charge on the 15th of January; they had lodged with me from the 29th or 30th of October, and the goods had been let them with their lodging.
JAMES SPENCER. I am a pawnbroker. I have a piece of patchwork and a looking-glass, which I took in of a female on the 4th and 5th of November.
WILLIAM BROOKS. I am a pawnbroker - I live in Bulstrode-street. I have two pillows and a bolster pawned on the 30th of October, for 3s., by the male prisoner - I am sure he is the person; I had seen him before - the prosecutor could not identify them before the Magistrate.
RICHARD HARTLEY WALL. I am an officer. I took the prisoners on another charge; I found this duplicate in a box in the room which they occupied.
Elizabeth Mitchell. My brother went to Mr. Brooks, and he refused to take some things of him on account of his youth, and then I took them - I have pawned things there, and taken them out again.
WILLIAM BROOKS. Yes, she has, but she did not pawn these things; the male prisoner did, and said they were for his sister.
Elizabeth Mitchell's Defence. I intended to redeem these things - I have stripped myself of every thing to support my brother, whom I have brought up; I could not turn him into the street - I have been three days without food, and always paid my rent.
JOHN CRAWFORD. They paid the rent, but I believe they pawned my things to do it - her brother was not present when I let her the lodging.
RICHARD HARTLEY WALL. When I went into the room the bed was stripped, and they were in the greatest distress - there were fifty-four duplicates in the box.
The prisoners received a good character.
E. MITCHELL - GUILTY. Aged 28.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury.
Fined One Shilling and Discharged.
L.J. MITCHELL. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18310217-232]
658. HUGH SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of February, 1 pair of trousers, value 12s.; 1 coat, value 20s.; 2 shirts, value 6s.; 1 waistcoat, value8s., and 20 pieces of ivory, value 14s., the goods of Charles Houlden.
CHARLES HOULDEN. I live in Rawstorne-street, and am an ivory-turner - the prisoner was my foreman for about twelve months. On the 14th of February I missed a coat, waistcoat, trousers, and two shirts - I got the officer, who took the prisoner; he denied it, but I told him I had seen the articles - I then went to his lodging, at No. 28, Rawstorne-street; he then delivered up to the officer two shirts, a pair of trousers, and a waistcoat - he then went to another room, unlocked a trunk, and gave out a coat - I know the coat, waistcoat, and trousers are mine, and I believe the shirts are; the officer took some pieces of ivory from a trunk, and some from a drawer in the room.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Had he not been employed by you before this last time? A. Yes - I know part of his family; I believe they are respectable.
JOHN MURPHY. I am a Police-officer. I took the prisoner on the 15th of January - I went to his lodgings with him, and the prosecutor followed us there - the prisoner took some things from a drawer; I found these ivory articles in the bottom of the chest of drawers - he then produced this coat from a trunk, and I found some more ivory there.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 23.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18310217-233]
659. JANE ROBINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of January, 6 yards of lawn, value 9s., the goods of Thomas Mitchell.
THOMAS ASHMORE. I live with Mr. Thomas Mitchell. On the 10th of January I was in his shop, and saw the prisoner come in with another female - they asked for some worsted stockings; I turned to get them, and in turning I saw the prisoner take from the counter a piece of lawn, which she put behind her - I accused her of it, and asked where it was; she dropped it behind her, and I picked it up - this is it.
Cross-examined by MR. STURGEON. Q. Did you see her drop it? A. Yes, I saw it fall behind her - it had been about one-third of the way on the counter - the other woman went away when the prisoner took the lawn.
Prisoner. It laid near the edge, and I knocked it off in reaching for some handkerchiefs. Witness. I am sure she took it off, and when I spoke to her, she dropped it.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18310217-234]
660. CHARLES ROBSON was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of January, 2 reams of paper, value 12s., the goods of Thomas Elborough.
ELIZA JONES ELBOROUGH. I live with my brother, Thomas Elborough, a baker, in Cross-street, Islington. On the 24th of January, I was in his shop - the prisoner came, and said he had brought some bags which my brother had ordered, and he had left some paper with my brother, which he wanted; I said, "I don't know any thing about the paper, you must wait till my brother returns;" he said he must have the paper, and pointed to a bundle of whitey-brown paper, which was in view at the time - he did not say whether my brother had paid for that paper or not; he remained about half an hour, and said he must have the paper - I still said he must wait for my brother, but he said he must have it, and I at last said,"You must take it, if it is yours," and he took it away; I had seen him frequently before, and know he is the person.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not say I had brought some bags your brother had ordered, and you gave me a quire of paper to make up into pound bags size? A. He took a quire of paper, and cut some out, which he left in the shop.
THOMAS ELBOROUGH. I left my sister in care of the shop - on my return she stated this circumstance; I have bought bags of the prisoner, but he had never left any bundle of paper there, and I had not ordered any bags then.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you order me to bring some bags, and tell me to cut out some bags for you? A. No, not at that time - I have ordered bags.
CHARLES KNOTT. I am a Police-serjeant. I took the prisoner at lslington, in consequence of information - he said he thought there must be some mistake, and he had no knowledge of this shop; I asked him about all the other baker's shops in lslington, and he knew them all but the prosecutor's.
The prisoner pleaded distress.
GUILTY. Aged 28. - Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18310217-235]
661. EDWARD PAYNE was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of January, 1 plane, value 10s., the goods of Robert Fentham; and 1 plane, value 2s., the goods of William Richardson .
WILLIAM SMITH. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Lower-street, Islington. On the evening of the 20th of January, the prisoner brought this plough plane to pawn; I questioned him - he said he was sent to pawn it by a person named Smith, in Paradise-row; I told him to fetch the man - he went away, returned, and said the person was very busy - he could not come that night, and I must give him the plane again; I said the person might come in the morning - I detained the plane; he went away, and did not return.
ROBERT FEATHAM. I am a carpenter, and live in Brooksby-street, Islington. This plane is mine, and was lost from my shop in Barford-buildings, Liverpool-road, but in what way I cannot tell.
WILLIAM CARTWRIGHT. I keep a broker's shop. I bought this smoothing plane, on or about the 20th of January, of a boy, who I believe was the prisoner.
WILLIAM RICHARDSON. I am foreman to Mr. Featham. This plane is mine - I did not miss it till I was informed where it was; the prisoner's father works near our premises, and I have seen the prisoner come backwards and forwards very frequently - this plane might have been gone a week before I missed it.
GEORGE HAVILL. I took the prisoner - he denied the charge at first, but he afterwards said he took the plough plane out of the shop, and there was only one man at work at the time.
GUILTY. Aged 12.
Recommended to Mercy. - Whipped and Discharged.

662. ANN PAYNE was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of February, 7 lbs. weight of pork, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Zachariah Richard Catchpole.
ZACHARIAH RICHARD CATCHPOLE. I am a cheesemonger, and live in High-street, Shadwell. On the morning of the 17th of February I saw the prisoner near my shop window - I missed a hand of pork, which I had seen five minutes before; I went after the prisoner, and took her with it under her shawl.
Cross-examined by MR. HEATON. Q. Have you always told the same story? A. Yes; I never said I found it several yards from her - it had not been on the ground when I took it from her, but it dropped from my hand; it had been within my window - I did not hear the Magistrate refuse to commit her.
COURT. Q. Are you sure she had it under her shawl? A. Yes, she had a child in her arms - another woman had been asking the price of some pork.
WILLIAM SHARMAN. I am an officer. I took the prisoner, and have the pork.(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoner pleaded poverty, and received an excellent character.
GUILTY. Aged 38.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Months.

663. JOHN BARNES was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of January, 1 pair of trousers, value 2s., the goods of Hymon Levy.
HYMON LEVY. I am a slop-seller, in Broad-street, Ratcliff. I lost these trousers on the 27th of January from my door-post, but I was not at home.
CHARLES LEVY. I am eleven years old. I was at home, and the trousers were at our door - a great crowd came - a man came, took down the trousers, and ran into the crowd again; I lost sight of him - I saw the prisoner at the office on Saturday, and I know he is the man who took them - I am quite sure he is the person; I ran, and called Stop thief! but he got into the crowd - I saw his face.
SAMUEL WAKELING. I am a Police-constable. The prisoner is one of the sailors who was in a mob, in the highway - I took him at Ratcliff-cross, and found these trousers on him.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. We went to go to the Admiralty - many of us were without clothes; I picked up the trousers in the crowd.
GUILTY. Aged 23. - Transported for Seven Years.

664. WILLIAM PATTISON was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of February, 1 watch, value 31s.; 2 seals, value 30s., and 2 keys, value 1s., the goods of Ann Stubbs, his mistress.
ANN STUBBS. I live in Peerless-row, City-road. The prisoner was my errand-boy for two days - he went out with me about ten o'clock in the morning; I sent him back for something, and when I returned, the watch which I had left hanging up, was gone, with the seals and keys, from the back parlour.
EDITH WARWICK. I am servant to Mrs. Stubbs. I let the prisoner in when he returned, but did not see him take the watch - he went into the back parlour to warm his hands.
JOSEPH WILLIAM CLARK. I am servant to Mr. Fleming, a pawnbroker, of Farringdon-street. On the 3rd of February this watch and seals were pawned, but I do not know who by.
JOHN BROWN. I took the prisoner on Friday evening, two days after the robbery, and found the duplicate on him.
Prisoner's Defence. I never had the watch.
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Transported for Seven Years.

665. GEORGE NORMAN and JOSEPH PORTER were indicted for stealing, on the 16th of February, 4 bushels of malt, value 35s., the goods of Francis Nash.
MR. HEATON conducted the prosecution.
JOHN ROVE. I am clerk to Francis Nash, of Pickle Herring-wharf, Tooley-street. Norman was his carter, and on the 16th of February I directed him to load forty sacks of malt to go to Mr. West's - I saw them loaded and counted them in the waggon; I saw Norman leave the premises with them - Porter was not with him then: thirty-nine of the sacks were marked, "J. Collins," and one" F. Collins."
JOHN AYRES. I am occasionally employed by Mr. West. On the 16th of February I was at his brewery in John-street; the prisoners came with a waggon, and said they had brought twenty quarters of malt - they left twelve sacks there; Porter brought them all in - I saw him count them, and counted them myself; the empty sacks were put on the waggon, but not tied.
THOMAS YATES. I am servant to Mr. West, at his brewery, Turner-square, Hoxton. The prisoners came there with a waggon, and said they had brought fourteen quarters of malt - I attended to the delivery; after Porter had brought in six quarters I saw that he had an empty sack doubled up on his shoulder, and a full one on it - when he had shot that I took the empty one, and asked if he had brought two sacks at once - he said his mate had thrown it on his shoulder; after delivering four or five more sacks he took the empty sack, and said he would throw it into the waggon - master came up, and I told him what had occurred; he counted the empty sacks - there were twenty-eight, which was right; I should think an empty sack on his shoulder would be an hindrance to him.
JAMES WILLIAM WEST. I was at Turner-square - I did not measure what was delivered.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18310217-240]
666. RICHARD MOXAM was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of October, 84 dozens of fire-works, called crackers, value 28s., the goods of Charles Cockett.
CHARLES COCKETT. I am a bedstead-maker - my wife makes fire-works, at No. 10, Willow-gardens, but we do not live there - I have known the prisoner ten years, and employed him; I lost these fire-works - he left, and I had him taken.
GEORGE KEMP. I am a Policeman. I could not find the prisoner till the 14th of February - he had absconded; I got these fire-works from Shoreditch workhouse, and some from Gloucester-court.
FRANCIS DAVIS. I am cook at Shoreditch workhouse.One Sunday morning, between nine and ten o'clock, the prisoner brought me this parcel - I did not know what it was; I put it up stairs, and afterwards gave it to Kemp, but it had then been opened and some were taken out by the boys.
PAUL PRICE. I met the prisoner in Kingsland-road - he gave me a parcel to carry to Whitecross-street, and gave me 2d.(Property produced and sworn to.)

667. JOSEPH MARTIN was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of February, 2 sacks, value 4s., the goods of John Smith.

JOHN HOLLAND. I am a Policeman. I met the prisoner about seven o'clock in the morning, on the 12th of February, with a bag, and asked what was in it - he said rags and bones; I made him turn it out, and he had these two sacks - he said he found one behind a house at Camden-town, he could not tell where - this was three-quarters of a mile from Smith's - I found out the prosecutor; the prisoner then said he had them from there.(Property produced and sworn to.)

668. JOHN IRELAND was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of January, 1 hat, value 16s, the goods of Jeremiah Spiller.
JAREMIAH SPILLER. I am a mahogany-polisher, and live in Carnaby-market. On the 23rd of January I was locked out of my lodging, and went to look for one; I saw a great many people at the door of the Crown public-house, Pulteney-street - I asked for a lodging there; I was returning home; a man in a flannel jacket ran against me, and as he passed me he took off my hat - I was running after him, and another man knocked me down; I should know him again - I went and saw a Policeman.
WILLIAM ELLIS. I am a Policeman. I was going on duty at half-past six o'clock on Sunday morning, and saw about twenty-five people about the Crown, making a disturbance - I went up to them, and saw the prisoner with this hat just stuck on his head; it did not seem to fit - I took it off, and asked where he got it; he said, "What is that to you?" I asked the maker's name, and the colour of the lining; he said he could not tell - I was taking him to the station; he ran away - another Policeman took him; he was dressed in a flannel jacket.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I had been all night at a wake - a man came and asked me to buy a hat; I gave him 4s. for it.
GUILTY. Aged 23. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18310217-243]
669. MARY ANN HARBOUR was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of January, 2 half-crowns, 7 shillings, and 1 sixpence, the monies of Thomas Smith, from his person.
THOMAS SMITH. I am carter to a gentleman in the Edgware-road. On the night of the 22nd of January, I went to the Horse and Sack public-house, between ten and eleven o'clock - I was quite sober; I had two pints of half-and-half there - the prisoner was there; she asked me for beer - I gave her some; she asked for gin, and I told the boy to bring a quartern; she stood by me, and I felt her hand in my trousers pocket; (I had seen her two years ago, but not since) I had 12s. 6d. in my pocket - she pulled it all out; I saw it in her hand -I tried to get it from her, but she threw it down in one corner; I saw two half-crowns and one shilling in her hand - there was a parcel of chaps in the room; I was afraid to look for the money - the people then shoved me about, and I got away as fast as I could; I am certain my money was safe when I went into the house - I never got any of it back; I went out for a Policeman, and when I returned the house was all shut up, and she was gone; I had been away a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes.
JURY. Q. Could you hear any body in the house after you returned? A. No - I knocked, and nobody answered.
THOMAS SHILVER. I am pot-boy at the Horse and Sack, kept by Mr. Moss. The prisoner came in on this Saturday night, before the prosecutor - he sat in the taproom for a minute, and then stood up by the fire; I saw the prisoner speak to him; he called for a quartern of gin, and gave me half a crown - I gave him the change; the prisoner then put her hand into his pocket, and pulled out the money; the men who had been drinking with her were laughing and talking together, and she dropped all but two half-crowns and one shilling, which she kept in her hand - the prosecutor accused her of the robbery; my master was serving in the bar, and knew nothing of it till morning - the house was cleared because it was twelve o'clock - I believe the prisoner went away with the men; I found no money on the floor - I believe the men picked it up.
Prisoner's Defence. I met him by the Red Lion; he took me to this house, and paid for some fish for me there - I heard some money drop, but do not know where it came from; I got intoxicated, and do not know what became of him.
GUILTY. Aged 24. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18310217-244]

JAMES GADSEN. I live in New Nichol-street, Bethnal-green, and am a weaver - the prisoner is my son. On the 11th of February he was at home, and next morning I missed my great coat from the front kitchen; I never allowed him to pawn my things - I am obliged to prosecute him, as I can keep nothing.
THOMAS COOMBS. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Church-street, Bethnal-green. I have a coat, pawned on the 13th of February; to the best of my belief by the prisoner.
MORGAN WATKINS. I am a Policeman. I was called to take the prisoner, and found the duplicate on him.

671. JAMES EDWARDS was indicted for stealing,on the 19th of February, 1 spade, value 2s., the goods of George Meritt.
GEORGE MERITT. I am a labourer, and live at Finchley. On the 19th of February I saw the prisoner at Lord Mansfield's-wood, with some wood; I asked if he wanted a job; he said, Yes - I asked if he could do ditching - he said a little, but he had no spade, nor money to buy one; he came to work on Saturday morning, but it being wet, he was late, and I told him he must come earlier in future - I borrowed a spade, and set him to work - it was breakfast time; he said he had no money nor breakfast - I said, "Well, sixpence is no object - I have a quartern loaf at home, and you shall have half of it;" I went home, returned in twenty minutes, and the spade I had lent him was stuck in the ground, but my spade was gone, and him also - I went and told a Policeman, and he was taken; it was my master's spade, but I am answerable for it.
VALENTINE HARRISON. I am a Policeman. I took the prisoner on the same day as I received information; he was at Kentish-town, going towards London, with the spade.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Confined Six Months.

GUILTY. Aged 16. - Confined Three Weeks.
